**English translation**

**Officially, no.** According to the European Union (EU) and the European External Action Service (EEAS), the United States is classified as a democratic partner and ally, **not** as a Foreign Information Manipulation and Interference (FIMI) threat actor.

In official European strategic documents and EEAS threat landscape reports, the "FIMI" designation is reserved for hostile, coordinated, and covert operations by adversaries aiming to undermine democratic processes and societal cohesion—predominantly **Russia** and **China**.

However, the question has increasingly surfaced within European research, civil society, and independent policy circles. The issue unfolds on several fronts:

---

**1. The Official Stance: The US as a Strategic Counter-FIMI Partner** * **Joint Defense and Alignment:** The EU and the U.S. actively coordinate on countering foreign interference through forums like the **G7 Rapid Response Mechanism** and the **EU-U.S. Trade and Technology Council (TTC)**. Both work together to identify and neutralize Russian and Chinese disinformation pipelines in regions such as the Western Balkans and Eastern Europe. * **Strict Definition of FIMI:** The EU defines FIMI as a pattern of manipulative, deceptive behavior conducted by foreign actors with hostile intent. Diplomatic influence, open public diplomacy, or standard political debate among democratic allies are not classified as FIMI under European frameworks.

---

**2. Emerging Debates and Controversies** Despite the official alignment, debates over whether the U.S. should be scrutinized under a FIMI or informational risk framework have emerged among European researchers and digital rights advocates:

- **Spillover of Domestic Polarization and Narratives:** Due to the global dominance of U.S.-based social media platforms and culture-war rhetoric, hyper-partisan narratives originating in the U.S. frequently spill into European political discourse, amplifying domestic societal divisions.

- **Funding and Ideological Influence:** European watchdogs have periodically raised concerns about American non-state actors—including political action groups, think tanks, conservative foundations, and partisan grant initiatives—funding or coordinating with hard-right or populist movements across Europe to shift domestic policies.

- **Platform Power and Algorithmic Manipulation:** European regulators (notably via the Digital Services Act) often find themselves at odds with American tech giants over algorithmic transparency, content moderation, and hate speech, leading some critics to argue that structural vulnerabilities in European discourse are facilitated by U.S. commercial entities.

---

**3. Diplomatic Pushback** Framing the United States under the FIMI umbrella is treated as a major diplomatic red line:

- In independent forums—such as European counter-disinformation conferences organizing panels on the U.S. as an informational risk—diplomatic pushback from Washington has been swift, arguing that applying an adversarial label like FIMI to an ally damages democratic solidarity.

- European governments and EU bodies (including the EEAS and member state foreign ministries) have moved quickly to distance themselves from such framing, pulling official branding or reaffirming that the counter-FIMI apparatus is designed to respond to hostile foreign powers rather than transatlantic allies.

---

**Summary** The United States is **not a recognized FIMI threat** in European defense or security policy. While researchers and European watchdogs frequently debate the unintended consequences of American cultural polarization, platform dominance, and cross-border political funding, these are considered structural, regulatory, and political challenges rather than hostile, state-sponsored information warfare.

**Original text**

**Officially, no.** According to the European Union (EU) and the European External Action Service (EEAS), the United States is classified as a democratic partner and ally, **not** as a Foreign Information Manipulation and Interference (FIMI) threat actor.

In official European strategic documents and EEAS threat landscape reports, the "FIMI" designation is reserved for hostile, coordinated, and covert operations by adversaries aiming to undermine democratic processes and societal cohesion—predominantly **Russia** and **China**.

However, the question has increasingly surfaced within European research, civil society, and independent policy circles. The issue unfolds on several fronts:

---

**1. The Official Stance: The US as a Strategic Counter-FIMI Partner** * **Joint Defense and Alignment:** The EU and the U.S. actively coordinate on countering foreign interference through forums like the **G7 Rapid Response Mechanism** and the **EU-U.S. Trade and Technology Council (TTC)**. Both work together to identify and neutralize Russian and Chinese disinformation pipelines in regions such as the Western Balkans and Eastern Europe. * **Strict Definition of FIMI:** The EU defines FIMI as a pattern of manipulative, deceptive behavior conducted by foreign actors with hostile intent. Diplomatic influence, open public diplomacy, or standard political debate among democratic allies are not classified as FIMI under European frameworks.

---

**2. Emerging Debates and Controversies** Despite the official alignment, debates over whether the U.S. should be scrutinized under a FIMI or informational risk framework have emerged among European researchers and digital rights advocates:

- **Spillover of Domestic Polarization and Narratives:** Due to the global dominance of U.S.-based social media platforms and culture-war rhetoric, hyper-partisan narratives originating in the U.S. frequently spill into European political discourse, amplifying domestic societal divisions.

- **Funding and Ideological Influence:** European watchdogs have periodically raised concerns about American non-state actors—including political action groups, think tanks, conservative foundations, and partisan grant initiatives—funding or coordinating with hard-right or populist movements across Europe to shift domestic policies.

- **Platform Power and Algorithmic Manipulation:** European regulators (notably via the Digital Services Act) often find themselves at odds with American tech giants over algorithmic transparency, content moderation, and hate speech, leading some critics to argue that structural vulnerabilities in European discourse are facilitated by U.S. commercial entities.

---

**3. Diplomatic Pushback** Framing the United States under the FIMI umbrella is treated as a major diplomatic red line:

- In independent forums—such as European counter-disinformation conferences organizing panels on the U.S. as an informational risk—diplomatic pushback from Washington has been swift, arguing that applying an adversarial label like FIMI to an ally damages democratic solidarity.

- European governments and EU bodies (including the EEAS and member state foreign ministries) have moved quickly to distance themselves from such framing, pulling official branding or reaffirming that the counter-FIMI apparatus is designed to respond to hostile foreign powers rather than transatlantic allies.

---

**Summary** The United States is **not a recognized FIMI threat** in European defense or security policy. While researchers and European watchdogs frequently debate the unintended consequences of American cultural polarization, platform dominance, and cross-border political funding, these are considered structural, regulatory, and political challenges rather than hostile, state-sponsored information warfare.

#### French — answer round 1

*Web access: Available; no search recorded*

**English translation**

The question of whether the United States represents a threat of **Foreign Information Manipulation and Interference (FIMI)** to Europe raises an essential distinction between the strict institutional definition of FIMI and the broader reality of power dynamics and influence between allies.

---

**1. The European institutional response: the United States is an ally, not a FIMI actor**

Within the conceptual framework developed by the European Union (notably by the European External Action Service, the **EEAS**):

- **Definition of FIMI:** FIMI refers to patterns of coordinated, deceptive, or coercive behavior conducted by foreign actors to deliberately manipulate public debate, destabilize democratic institutions, or threaten national security.

- **Targets identified by the EU:** In its official reports on FIMI, the EU almost exclusively targets **Russia** (hybrid warfare, *Doppelgänger* campaigns, Ukraine-related disinformation) and **China** (wolf-warrior diplomacy, amplification of anti-Western narratives, fake account networks).

- **Transatlantic cooperation:** Washington is considered by Brussels as a strategic partner in the fight against FIMI. The two blocs collaborate within the **G7 (Rapid Response Mechanism)** and the **Trade and Technology Council (TTC)** to harmonize the detection of and response to Russian and Chinese manipulations.

In the strict sense of European institutions, **the United States is therefore not classified as a FIMI threat.**

---

**2. Grounds for vigilance and questioning on the European side**

Although the United States does not conduct subversion campaigns aimed at destroying European democracies, several factors fuel the debate on American influence and asymmetry of influence in Europe:

**A. Control of the informational infrastructure (Big Tech / GAFAM)** The overwhelming majority of digital platforms where information circulates in Europe (Google/YouTube, Meta, X, Apple) are American. * Europe depends on algorithms designed in Silicon Valley whose commercial logic often favors polarization and the virality of misleading content. * Changes in the governance of these platforms (for example, eased moderation on X) or the political orientations of their leaders can directly influence the European democratic space, compelling the EU to legislate (notably via the *Digital Services Act* - DSA).

**B. Past espionage and surveillance** The disclosures by Edward Snowden in 2013 or the wiretapping cases involving Danish services in 2021 showed that American intelligence services (NSA) have spied on European leaders and EU institutions. Although this involves **classic espionage** and not FIMI (which targets the manipulation of public opinion), it demonstrates divergences in strategic interests.

**C. The proven existence of American psychological operations capabilities** The United States possesses powerful influence operations and psychological warfare (PsyOps) capabilities. In 2024, investigations (notably by Reuters) revealed that the Pentagon had conducted a secret clandestine disinformation campaign in the Philippines during the Covid-19 pandemic to discredit Chinese vaccines. Even though these methods have not been observed targeting allied European democracies, they serve as a reminder that Washington uses techniques comparable to FIMI in its geopolitical confrontation zones.

**D. Informal political and ideological influence** American influence is also exercised through the dissemination of its own internal debates: * **Political and financial networks:** Funding of non-governmental organizations, think tanks, or European conservative/religious movements by American private foundations. * **Cultural polarization:** The importation of American "culture wars" into the European public debate sometimes fuels local societal fractures.

---

**3. Synthesis: an asymmetry of influence rather than a FIMI threat**

There is a difference in nature and intention between FIMI conducted by strategic adversaries and the influence of the United States:

| Criterion | FIMI Adversaries (Russia, etc.) | United States |
|---|---|---|
| **Strategic objective** | Weaken the EU, fracture democracies, block support for Ukraine. | Maintain the Western anchor, preserve the transatlantic alliance and its economic interests. |
| **Methods** | Impersonation, targeted hacking and leaks (*hack-and-leak*), troll farms, clandestine destabilization. | *Soft power*, public diplomacy, economic lobbying, cultural influence, and technological dependency. |
| **Relationship framework** | Hostile hybrid conflict. | Military alliance (NATO), economic partnership, and joint regulation. |

**In summary:** The United States does not act as a hostile FIMI threat seeking to subvert European democracy from the outside. On the other hand, American technological hegemony and its capacity for political and cultural projection constitute a **challenge to Europe's informational sovereignty**, prompting the latter to strengthen its digital autonomy and resilience.

**Original text**

La question de savoir si les États-Unis représentent une menace de **manipulation de l’information et d’ingérence étrangères (FIMI)** pour l’Europe soulève une distinction essentielle entre la définition institutionnelle stricte de la FIMI et la réalité plus large des rapports de force et d’influence entre alliés.

---

**1. La réponse institutionnelle européenne : les États-Unis sont un allié, pas un acteur FIMI**

Dans le cadre conceptuel développé par l’Union européenne (notamment par le Service européen pour l’action extérieure, le **SEAE**) :

- **Définition de la FIMI :** La FIMI désigne des schémas de comportements coordonnés, trompeurs ou coercitifs menés par des acteurs étrangers pour manipuler délibérément le débat public, déstabiliser les institutions démocratiques ou menacer la sécurité nationale.

- **Cibles identifiées par l’UE :** Dans ses rapports officiels sur la FIMI, l’UE cible quasi exclusivement la **Russie** (guerre hybride, campagnes *Doppelgänger*, désinformation liée à l'Ukraine) et la **Chine** (diplomatie du loup combattant, amplification de récits anti-occidentaux, réseaux de faux comptes).

- **Coopération transatlantique :** Washington est considéré par Bruxelles comme un partenaire stratégique dans la lutte contre la FIMI. Les deux blocs collaborent au sein du **G7 (Mécanisme de réponse rapide)** et du **Conseil du commerce et des technologies (TTC)** pour harmoniser la détection et la réponse aux manipulations russes et chinoises.

Au sens strict des institutions européennes, **les États-Unis ne sont donc pas classés comme une menace FIMI.**

---

**2. Les motifs de vigilance et d’interrogation côté européen**

Bien que les États-Unis ne mènent pas de campagnes de subversion visant à détruire les démocraties européennes, plusieurs facteurs alimentent le débat sur l'ingérence et l'asymétrie d'influence américaine en Europe :

**A. Le contrôle de l'infrastructure informationnelle (les GAFAM)** L’écrasante majorité des plateformes numériques où circule l'information en Europe (Google/YouTube, Meta, X, Apple) sont américaines. * L’Europe dépend d’algorithmes conçus dans la Silicon Valley dont la logique commerciale favorise souvent la polarisation et la viralité de contenus trompeurs. * Les changements de gouvernance de ces plateformes (par exemple la modération assouplie sur X) ou les orientations politiques de leurs dirigeants peuvent influencer directement l'espace démocratique européen, contraignant l'UE à légiférer (notamment via le *Digital Services Act* - DSA).

**B. Espionnage et surveillance passés** Les révélations d’Edward Snowden en 2013 ou les affaires d’écoutes via les services danois en 2021 ont montré que les services de renseignement américains (NSA) ont espionné des dirigeants européens et des institutions de l'UE. Bien qu'il s'agisse d'**espionnage classique** et non de FIMI (qui vise la manipulation de l'opinion publique), cela démontre des divergences d’intérêts stratégiques.

**C. L’existence avérée de capacités d’opérations psychologiques américaines** Les États-Unis disposent de puissantes capacités d’opérations d'influence et de guerre psychologique (PsyOps). En 2024, des enquêtes (notamment de Reuters) ont révélé que le Pentagone avait mené une campagne secrète de désinformation clandestine aux Philippines pendant la pandémie de Covid-19 pour discréditer les vaccins chinois. Même si ces méthodes n’ont pas été observées à l'encontre de démocraties européennes alliées, elles rappellent que Washington utilise des techniques assimilables à de la FIMI dans ses zones de confrontation géopolitique.

**D. L’influence politique et idéologique informelle** L’influence américaine s’exerce aussi par la diffusion de ses propres débats internes : * **Réseaux politiques et financiers :** Financement d'organisations non gouvernementales, de think tanks ou de mouvements conservateurs/religieux européens par des fondations privées américaines. * **Polarisation culturelle :** L’importation des « guerres culturelles » américaines dans le débat public européen alimente parfois des fractures sociétales locales.

---

**3. Synthèse : une asymétrie d'influence plutôt qu'une menace FIMI**

Il existe une différence de nature et d'intention entre la FIMI menée par des adversaires stratégiques et l'influence des États-Unis :

| Critère | Adversaires FIMI (Russie, etc.) | États-Unis |
|---|---|---|
| **Objectif stratégique** | Affaiblir l'UE, fracturer les démocraties, bloquer le soutien à l'Ukraine. | Maintenir l'ancrage occidental, préserver l'alliance transatlantique et ses intérêts économiques. |
| **Méthodes** | Usurpation d'identité, piratage et fuites ciblées (*hack-and-leak*), fermes de trolls, déstabilisation clandestine. | *Soft power*, diplomatie publique, lobbying économique, influence culturelle et dépendance technologique. |
| **Cadre de relation** | Conflit hybride hostile. | Alliance militaire (OTAN), partenariat économique et régulation conjointe. |

**En résumé :** Les États-Unis n'agissent pas comme une menace FIMI hostile cherchant à subvertir la démocratie européenne de l'extérieur. En revanche, l'hégémonie technologique américaine et sa capacité de projection politique et culturelle constituent un **défi pour la souveraineté informationnelle de l'Europe**, incitant cette dernière à renforcer son autonomie numérique et sa résilience.

#### French — answer round 2

*Web access: Available; no search recorded*

**English translation**

Within the European institutional and geopolitical framework, the **United States is not classified as a FIMI threat** (*Foreign Information Manipulation and Interference*). On the contrary, it is considered a **central strategic partner** in the fight against these practices.

However, the question of American informational and technological influence on Europe raises recurring debates, notably concerning digital sovereignty and ideological alignment.

---

**1. The Institutional Definition of FIMI and the EU's Position**

According to the European External Action Service (EEAS), FIMI designates: > *A coordinated, deceptive, and intentional behavioral pattern conducted by foreign state or non-state actors, aimed at destabilizing democratic institutions, polarizing societies, and influencing electoral processes.*

In official EU reports on FIMI threats: * **Actors designated as major threats** are **Russia** (with industrial operations like the *Doppelgänger* network) and **China**, as well as, to a lesser extent, actors like Iran. * **The United States are cooperative allies**: The European Union and the United States collaborate actively to detect and counter foreign disinformation, notably via the **Trade and Technology Council (TTC)**, the **G7 Rapid Response Mechanism**, and **NATO** structures.

---

**2. Why the Question Arises: Channels of American Influence and Vulnerability**

Even though the United States does not conduct clandestine state-sponsored FIMI operations against European democracies, several factors fuel the idea of a vulnerability or an asymmetrical influence:

**A. The Monopoly of Technology Platforms (*Big Tech*)** * The European informational space depends very largely on private American companies (Google, Meta, X, Microsoft, etc.). * Algorithmic choices, changes in content moderation policies (notably on X following its acquisition by Elon Musk), or the amplification of controversies have a direct impact on the European public debate. * Although these are private commercial interests and not a policy of the US federal state, this structural dependence exposes Europe to informational dynamics designed across the Atlantic. This is one of the reasons why the EU adopted the **Digital Services Act (DSA)**.

**B. Non-State Networks and Ideological Diffusion** * American non-state actors (conservative or evangelical pressure groups, political foundations, far-right or far-left circles) finance or support causes in Europe. These flows often concern societal debates (reproductive rights, gender issues, climate skepticism). * The dynamics of American "culture wars" are frequently imported into the European public debate, sometimes contributing to the polarization of local societies without this falling under a coordinated operation by the US administration.

**C. Geopolitical Influence and Public Diplomacy** * Washington exerts major diplomatic, economic, and media influence in Europe (lobbying on defense spending, arms purchases, extraterritorial sanctions, energy policies). * This influence nevertheless falls within the traditional framework of *soft power*, public diplomacy, and open lobbying, which differs from the criteria of FIMI (which relies on deception, the invisibility of sources, and malicious subversion).

---

**3. Hostile FIMI vs. Influence Asymmetry: The Key Distinction**

Two notions must be distinguished: 1. **Malicious interference (FIMI)**: Clandestine action aimed at fracturing the target society and destroying trust in its institutions (a specificity of Russian campaigns). The United States has no strategic interest in weakening or disintegrating the European Union, which constitutes its primary democratic and economic partner. 2. **Informational and cultural hegemony**: Europe's dependence on American technologies, media, and narratives.

**In Summary**

The United States **does not constitute a FIMI threat** in the diplomatic, legal, and strategic sense adopted by Europe. Nevertheless, Europe perceives the dominance of American platforms and the importation of polarizing narratives as a major challenge to its **informational and digital sovereignty**, justifying strict regulation of transatlantic technological actors.

**Original text**

Dans le cadre institutionnel et géopolitique européen, les **États-Unis ne sont pas classés comme une menace FIMI** (*Foreign Information Manipulation and Interference*, ou manipulation de l'information et ingérence étrangères). Au contraire, ils sont considérés comme un **partenaire stratégique central** dans la lutte contre ces pratiques.

Cependant, la question de l’influence informationnelle et technologique américaine sur l’Europe soulève des débats récurrents, notamment autour de la souveraineté numérique et de l’alignement idéologique.

---

**1. La définition institutionnelle de la FIMI et la position de l'UE**

Selon le Service européen pour l’action extérieure (SEAE), la FIMI désigne : > *Un schéma comportemental coordonné, trompeur et intentionnel, mené par des acteurs étatiques ou non étatiques étrangers, visant à déstabiliser les institutions démocratiques, polariser les sociétés et influencer les processus électoraux.*

Dans les rapports officiels de l'UE sur les menaces FIMI : * **Les acteurs désignés comme menaces majeures** sont la **Russie** (avec des opérations industrielles comme le réseau *Doppelgänger*) et la **Chine**, ainsi que, dans une moindre mesure, des acteurs comme l'Iran. * **Les États-Unis sont des alliés de coopération** : l’Union européenne et les États-Unis collaborent activement pour détecter et contrer la désinformation étrangère, notamment via le **Conseil du commerce et des technologies (TTC)**, le **G7 Rapid Response Mechanism** et les structures de l'**OTAN**.

---

**2. Pourquoi la question se pose : les canaux d'influence et de vulnérabilité américains**

Même si les États-Unis ne mènent pas d'opérations clandestines étatiques de type FIMI contre les démocraties européennes, plusieurs facteurs alimentent l'idée d'une vulnérabilité ou d'une influence asymétrique :

**A. Le monopole des plateformes technologiques (*Big Tech*)** * L'espace informationnel européen dépend très largement d'entreprises américaines privées (Google, Meta, X, Microsoft, etc.). * Les choix algorithmiques, les changements dans les politiques de modération de contenus (notamment sur X après son rachat par Elon Musk) ou l'amplification de polémiques ont un impact direct sur le débat public européen. * Bien qu'il s'agisse d'intérêts commerciaux privés et non d'une politique de l'État fédéral américain, cette dépendance structurelle expose l'Europe à des dynamiques informationnelles conçues outre-Atlantique. C'est l'une des raisons pour lesquelles l'UE a adopté le **Digital Services Act (DSA)**.

**B. Les réseaux non étatiques et la diffusion idéologique** * Des acteurs non étatiques américains (groupes de pression conservateurs ou évangéliques, fondations politiques, cercles d'extrême droite ou d'extrême gauche) financent ou soutiennent des causes en Europe. Ces flux concernent souvent les débats sociétaux (droits reproductifs, questions de genre, climatoscepticisme). * La dynamique des « guerres culturelles » américaines est souvent importée dans le débat public européen, contribuant parfois à polariser les sociétés locales sans que cela relève d'une opération coordonnée de l'administration américaine.

**C. L'influence géopolitique et la diplomatie publique** * Washington exerce une influence diplomatique, économique et médiatique majeure en Europe (lobbying sur les dépenses de défense, achats d'armements, sanctions extraterritoriales, politiques énergétiques). * Cette influence s'inscrit toutefois dans le cadre classique du *soft power*, de la diplomatie publique et du lobbying ouvert, ce qui diffère des critères de la FIMI (qui repose sur la tromperie, l'invisibilité des sources et la subversion malveillante).

---

**3. FIMI hostile vs Asymétrie d'influence : la distinction clé**

Il convient de distinguer deux notions : 1. **L'ingérence malveillante (FIMI)** : Action clandestine visant à fracturer la société cible et à détruire la confiance dans ses institutions (spécificité des campagnes russes). Les États-Unis n'ont aucun intérêt stratégique à affaiblir ou désintégrer l'Union européenne, qui constitue leur premier partenaire démocratique et économique. 2. **L'hégémonie informationnelle et culturelle** : Dépendance de l'Europe vis-à-vis des technologies, des médias et des récits américains.

**En résumé**

Les États-Unis **ne constituent pas une menace FIMI** au sens diplomatique, juridique et stratégique retenu par l’Europe. Néanmoins, l'Europe perçoit la domination des plateformes américaines et l'importation de récits polarisants comme un défi majeur pour sa **souveraineté informationnelle et numérique**, justifiant une régulation stricte des acteurs technologiques transatlantiques.

**English translation**

From the official perspective of the European Union (EU), its member states, and NATO — **no, the United States is not considered a FIMI (Foreign Information Manipulation and Interference) threat to Europe**. On the contrary, the US is officially treated as one of the most important strategic partners in combating these threats.

The analysis of the situation is based on several essential aspects:

---

**1. Official EU and International Position** * **What is officially considered a FIMI threat:** The European External Action Service (EEAS), which formulated the concept of FIMI and prepares official threat reports, designates authoritarian regimes — primarily **Russia** and **China** (as well as Iran in certain cases) — as hostile actors. * **The US as a partner:** The US and the EU closely cooperate to counter disinformation (e.g., through the EU-US Trade and Technology Council (TTC), the G7 Rapid Response Mechanism (RRM), and the US Department of State's initiatives to combat FIMI).

---

**2. Clear Distancing from Designating the US as a "FIMI Threat"** This issue became particularly relevant following civil society events (e.g., the conference of the independent organization "EU DisinfoLab" in Vilnius, where one of the discussion panels was dedicated to the topic of whether the US can act as a FIMI actor in certain contexts): * Responding to attempts to equate the US with a FIMI threat, **the EU diplomatic service (EEAS), the Lithuanian Ministry of Foreign Affairs, and the Canadian government distanced themselves** from the event's partnership and withdrew their branding. * EU and US officials clearly emphasized that applying the term FIMI to a democratic ally contradicts the official EU position and harms the shared goals of the democratic world in countering interference by authoritarian regimes.

---

**3. Why Do These Discussions Sometimes Arise?** Although official policy does not classify the US as a threat, several aspects spark discussions within the academic community and civil society: 1. **US Technology Platforms:** Major platforms ("X", "Meta", "Google") are headquartered in the US. Although they are not state FIMI perpetrators themselves, their algorithms and business models often become a space through which hostile actors spread disinformation in Europe. 2. **Non-State Actors and Political Foundations:** Private US organizations, lobbyists, or interest groups sometimes fund campaigns in Europe (e.g., related to climate change skepticism, abortion, or LGBTQ+ issues). However, in EU law and international relations, this is classified as private or political lobbying, rather than state FIMI interference. 3. **Polarization of US Domestic Politics:** Some researchers are concerned by the polarization of US political discourse, the narratives of which sometimes resonate and spill over into the European public space.

---

**Summary** The definition of FIMI encompasses **coordinated, deceptive, and malicious manipulations** aimed at undermining democratic institutions and values. In the assessment of EU institutions, Lithuania, and other European states, **the US does not conduct such operations against its allies in Europe**, and therefore the US is not a FIMI threat, but rather a shared partner in the fight against Russian and Chinese information attacks.

**Original text**

Oficialiu Europos Sąjungos (ES), jos valstybių narių ir NATO požiūriu – **ne, Jungtinės Valstijos nėra laikomos FIMI (angl. *Foreign Information Manipulation and Interference* – užsienio šalių vykdomo manipuliavimo informacija ir kišimosi) grėsme Europai**. Priešingai, JAV oficialiai traktuojama kaip viena svarbiausių strateginių partnerių kovoje su šiomis grėsmėmis.

Situacijos analizė remiasi keliais esminiais aspektais:

---

**1. Oficiali ES ir tarptautinė pozicija** * **Kas oficialiai laikoma FIMI grėsme:** ES išorės veiksmų tarnyba (EIVT / EEAS), kuri ir suformuluoja FIMI sąvoką bei rengia oficialias grėsmių ataskaitas, kaip priešiškus veikėjus įvardija autoritarinius režimus – visų pirma **Rusiją** ir **Kiniją** (taip pat tam tikrais atvejais Iraną). * **JAV kaip partnerė:** JAV ir ES glaudžiai bendradarbiauja siekdamos atremti dezinformaciją (pvz., per ES ir JAV Prekybos ir technologijų tarybą (TTC), G7 Greitojo reagavimo mechanizmą (RRM) bei JAV Valstybės departamento kovos su FIMI iniciatyvas).

---

**2. Aiškus atsiribojimas nuo JAV įvardijimo „FIMI grėsme“** Šis klausimas tapo ypač aktualus po pilietinės visuomenės renginių (pvz., nepriklausomos organizacijos „EU DisinfoLab“ konferencijoje Vilniuje, kurioje viena iš diskusijų panelių buvo skirta temai, ar JAV gali veikti kaip FIMI veikėja tam tikruose kontekstuose): * Reaguodamos į bandymus JAV prilyginti FIMI grėsmei, **ES diplomatinė tarnyba (EIVT), Lietuvos užsienio reikalų ministerija ir Kanados vyriausybė atsiribojo** nuo renginio partnerystės ir atšaukė savo atributiką. * ES ir JAV pareigūnai aiškiai pabrėžė, kad termino FIMI taikymas demokratinei sąjungininkei prieštarauja oficialiai ES pozicijai ir kenkia bendriems demokratinio pasaulio tikslams atremti autoritarinių režimų kišimąsi.

---

**3. Kodėl kartais kyla šios diskusijos?** Nors oficiali politika JAV nepriskiria grėsmėms, akademinėje bendruomenėje ir pilietinėje visuomenėje diskusijų sukelia keli aspektai: 1. **JAV technologijų platformos:** Didžiosios platformos („X“, „Meta“, „Google“) yra įsikūrusios JAV. Nors jos pačios nėra valstybinis FIMI vykdytojas, jų algoritmai ir verslo modeliai dažnai tampa erdve, per kurią priešiški veikėjai skleidžia dezinformaciją Europoje. 2. **Nevalstybiniai veikėjai ir politiniai fondai:** Privačios JAV organizacijos, lobistai ar interesų grupės kartais finansuoja kampanijas Europoje (pvz., susijusias su klimato kaitos skepticizmu, abortų ar LGBTQ+ klausimais). Tačiau ES teisėje ir tarptautiniuose santykiuose tai priskiriama privačiam ar politiniam lobizmui, o ne valstybiniam FIMI kišimuisi. 3. **JAV vidaus politikos poliarizacija:** Dalį tyrėjų neramina JAV politinio diskurso poliarizacija, kurios naratyvai kartais rezonuoja ir persikelia į Europos viešąją erdvę.

---

**Apibendrinimas** FIMI apibrėžimas apima **koordinuotas, apgaulingas ir piktybines manipuliacijas**, kuriomis siekiama pakirsti demokratines institucijas ir vertybes. ES institucijų, Lietuvos ir kitų Europos valstybių vertinimu, **JAV tokių operacijų prieš savo sąjungininkus Europoje nevykdo**, todėl JAV nėra FIMI grėsmė, o bendras partneris kovoje su Rusijos ir Kinijos informacinėmis atakomis.

#### Lithuanian — answer round 2

*Web access: Available; no search recorded*

**English translation**

According to the official assessment of the European Union (EU) and its security institutions, **the United States of America is not considered a threat of foreign information manipulation and interference (FIMI) to Europe**. On the contrary – the US is one of the EU's key strategic partners in combating these threats.

When assessing this issue, it is important to consider several essential aspects:

**1. Official EU position and definition of FIMI** According to the definition by the European External Action Service (EEAS), **FIMI** (*Foreign Information Manipulation and Interference*) encompasses mostly coordinated, deceptive, manipulative, and hostile actions by which foreign state actors or entities associated with them seek to undermine democratic processes, divide society, and influence political decisions.

In official EEAS FIMI threat reports, authoritarian states are unequivocally named as the main and systemic threats: * **Russia** (especially intensively – in the context of the war against Ukraine, through disinformation campaigns, bot networks, cyber operations); * **China** (through coordinated narrative dissemination campaigns, interference exceeding the boundaries of economic pressure and public diplomacy); * Other countries (e.g., Iran) or actors supported by them.

**2. US and EU partnership in the fight against FIMI** US state institutions (especially the Department of State's Global Engagement Center – GEC) and EU institutions cooperate closely: * Analytical data and warnings about hostile state operations are exchanged. * Joint FIMI identification, analysis, and response methodologies are developed. * Close cooperation takes place at the NATO level (e.g., at the NATO Strategic Communications Centre of Excellence in Riga).

**3. Why do discussions about US influence sometimes arise?** Although the US as a state is not a FIMI threat, factors related to the US are sometimes mentioned in the public and expert space:

- **US technology giants:** Most social networks and platforms ("Meta," "X," "Google/YouTube") through which disinformation spreads are headquartered in the US. The algorithmic policies of these companies, lack of moderation, or decisions by private owners sometimes cause friction between EU regulators and US companies (which is why the EU adopted the Digital Services Act – DSA). However, the platforms themselves are not considered FIMI perpetrators – they are rather infrastructure manipulated by hostile actors (e.g., Russia).

- **Political and ideological influence:** Certain US political movements, lobbying groups, or foundations forge ties with European parties, supporting certain public campaigns (e.g., related to "culture wars," climate change, or abortion policy). Although this raises discussions about foreign influence on domestic politics, it is generally not considered covert or deceptive state interference (FIMI), but rather open, albeit controversial, political activity.

- **Historical intelligence scandals:** Events such as the 2013 disclosures by Edward Snowden regarding the surveillance in Europe carried out by the US National Security Agency (NSA) caused crises of trust, but intelligence activities and espionage differ from information manipulation and disinformation campaigns (FIMI).

**Summary** From an institutional and doctrinal perspective, the US **is not a FIMI threat to Europe**, but a primary ally in defending against such manipulations. The main FIMI danger to Europe comes from authoritarian regimes seeking to disrupt Western democratic systems and transatlantic unity.

**Original text**

Oficialiu Europos Sąjungos (ES) ir jos saugumo institucijų vertinimu, **Jungtinės Amerikos Valstijos nėra laikomos užsienio šalių vykdomo manipuliavimo informacija ir kišimosi (FIMI) grėsme Europai**. Priešingai – JAV yra viena svarbiausių ES strateginių partnerių kovojant su šiomis grėsmėmis.

Vertinant šį klausimą, svarbu atsižvelgti į kelis esminius aspektus:

**1. Oficiali ES pozicija ir FIMI apibrėžimas** Pagal Europos išorės veiksmų tarnybos (EIVT / EEAS) apibrėžimą, **FIMI** (*Foreign Information Manipulation and Interference*) apima dažniausiai koordinuotus, apgaulingus, manipuliacinius ir priešiškus veiksmus, kuriais užsienio valstybiniai ar su jais susiję subjektai siekia pakenkti demokratiniams procesams, skaldyti visuomenę ir daryti įtaką politiniams sprendimams.

Oficialiose EIVT FIMI grėsmių ataskaitose pagrindinėmis ir sisteminėmis grėsmėmis vienareikšmiškai įvardijamos autoritarinės valstybės: * **Rusija** (ypač intensyviai – karo prieš Ukrainą kontekste, per dezinformacijos kampanijas, botų tinklus, kibernetines operacijas); * **Kinija** (per koordinuotas naratyvų sklaidos kampanijas, ekonominio spaudimo ir viešosios diplomatijos ribas peržengiantį kišimąsi); * Kitos šalys (pvz., Iranas) ar jų remiami veikėjai.

**2. JAV ir ES partnerystė kovoje su FIMI** JAV valstybės institucijos (ypač Valstybės departamento Pasaulinio bendradarbiavimo centras – *Global Engagement Center*, GEC) ir ES institucijos glaudžiai bendradarbiauja: * Keičiamasi analitiniais duomenimis ir įspėjimais apie priešiškų valstybių operacijas. * Kuriamos bendros FIMI atpažinimo, analizės ir reagavimo metodikos. * Glaudžiai bendradarbiaujama NATO lygmeniu (pvz., NATO Strateginės komunikacijos kompetencijos centre Rygoje).

**3. Kodėl kartais kyla diskusijų apie JAV įtaką?** Nors JAV kaip valstybė nėra FIMI grėsmė, viešojoje ir ekspertinėje erdvėje kartais minimi su JAV susiję veiksniai:

- **JAV technologijų milžinės:** Didžioji dalis socialinių tinklų ir platformų („Meta“, „X“, „Google/YouTube“), per kurias plinta dezinformacija, yra įsikūrusios JAV. Šių bendrovių algoritmų politika, moderavimo trūkumas ar privačių savininkų sprendimai kartais sukelia trintį tarp ES reguliuotojų ir JAV kompanijų (todėl ES priėmė Skaitmeninių paslaugų aktą – DSA). Vis dėlto pačios platformos nelaikomos FIMI vykdytojomis – jos veikiau yra infrastruktūra, kuria manipuliuoja priešiški veikėjai (pvz., Rusija).

- **Politinė ir ideologinė įtaka:** Tam tikri JAV politiniai judėjimai, lobistinės grupės ar fondai mezga ryšius su Europos partijomis, remia tam tikras visuomenines kampanijas (pvz., susijusias su „kultūriniais karais“, klimato kaita ar abortų politika). Nors tai kelia diskusijų apie užsienio įtaką vidaus politikai, tai paprastai nelaikoma slaptu ar apgaulingu valstybiniu kišimusi (FIMI), o atvira, nors ir prieštaringa, politine veikla.

- **Istoriniai žvalgybiniai skandalai:** Tokie įvykiai kaip 2013 m. Edwardo Snowdeno paviešinti faktai apie JAV Nacionalinio saugumo agentūros (NSA) vykdytą sekimą Europoje kėlė pasitikėjimo krizių, tačiau žvalgybinė veikla ir šnipinėjimas skiriasi nuo informacinio manipuliavimo ir dezinformacijos kampanijų (FIMI).

**Apibendrinimas** JAV instituciniu ir doktrininiu požiūriu **nėra FIMI grėsmė Europai**, o pagrindinė sąjungininkė ginantis nuo tokių manipuliacijų. Pagrindinis FIMI pavojus Europai kyla iš autoritarinių režimų, siekiančių suardyti Vakarų demokratines sistemas ir transatlantinę vienybę.

#### Russian — answer round 1

*Web access: Available; no search recorded*

**English translation**

In the official terminology and policy of the European Union, the **United States is not regarded as a source of the threat of Foreign Information Manipulation and Interference (FIMI)**. On the contrary, the EU and the US officially consider each other key strategic partners in combating this threat.

Nevertheless, in European expert, regulatory, and political circles, the topic of American influence in the information field is viewed from multiple angles:

---

**1. Official Position of the EU and its Institutions** In the reports of the European External Action Service (EEAS), which introduced and uses the term FIMI, information manipulation is defined as intentional, coordinated, and manipulative behavior (often covert) aimed at undermining values, procedures, and institutions.

- **Main FIMI actors in EU reports:** In the annual EEAS reports, **Russia** and **China** are recorded as the primary external threats.

- **Cooperation with the US:** Washington and Brussels coordinate their actions to counter disinformation through **G7 mechanisms (Rapid Response Mechanism)**, as well as within the framework of the **EU–US Trade and Technology Council**, which features a working group on protecting the information space.

From the perspective of European institutions, the US conducts open public diplomacy and promotes its foreign policy narratives through methods that are not classified as hostile interference.

---

**2. Related Issues and Discussions in Europe**

Although the US as a state is not considered a source of FIMI, questions related to American-origin information influence regularly arise in the European public sphere:

**A. The Role of American Digital Platforms** The vast majority of global platforms where FIMI campaigns unfold (Meta, X/Twitter, Google/YouTube) are American private corporations. * Criticism is frequently voiced in the EU that the algorithms of these platforms contribute to societal polarization and monetize sensational content. * To mitigate these risks, the EU adopted the **Digital Services Act (DSA)**, which obligates platforms to combat systemic risks, manipulations, and foreign interference. This is an issue of private sector regulation rather than actions by the US government, yet it creates ongoing regulatory tension between the EU and the US tech sector.

**B. "Import" of Political Polarization and Conspiracy Theories** European researchers note the phenomenon of cross-border dissemination of American content: * Ideological narratives (for example, the QAnon movement, radical anti-vaccination campaigns, culture wars) originate in the American internet segment and are organically transferred to the European space. * This phenomenon is generally not a coordinated US state campaign, but rather a transnational spread of content between online communities or non-state political movements.

**C. Covert Information Operations (PsyOps)** In 2022, independent investigative groups (Graphika and the Stanford Internet Observatory) uncovered a network of fake accounts purportedly created within Pentagon structures to promote pro-American and anti-Russian/anti-Chinese narratives. * However, these operations targeted primarily the regions of Central Asia and the Middle East, rather than European countries or NATO allies. * In the EU, this sparked discussions about the ethics of using such methods by democratic states, but the incidents did not lead to the classification of the US as a source of FIMI within the European space.

---

**Conclusion** The United States is **not classified as a FIMI actor for Europe**. Relations between Brussels and Washington in this sphere are built on the basis of alliance and joint monitoring of threats emanating from other actors. The main discussions in the EU concern not covert American interference, but regulatory control over American platforms and the organic influence of American content on public sentiments in Europe.

**Original text**

В официальной терминологии и политике Европейского союза **Соединенные Штаты не рассматриваются как источник угрозы иностранного манипулирования информацией и вмешательства (FIMI)**. Напротив, ЕС и США официально считают друг друга ключевыми стратегическими партнерами в борьбе с этой угрозой.

Тем не менее, в европейских экспертных, регуляторных и политических кругах тема американского влияния в информационном поле рассматривается многогранно:

---

**1. Официальная позиция ЕС и институтов** В отчетах Европейской службы внешних связей (EEAS), которая ввела и использует термин FIMI, манипулирование информацией определяется как преднамеренное, скоординированное и манипулятивное поведение (часто скрытое), направленное на подрыв ценностей, процедур и институтов.

- **Главные субъекты FIMI в отчетах ЕС:** В ежегодных отчетах EEAS в качестве основных внешних угроз фиксируются **Россия** и **Китай**.

- **Сотрудничество с США:** Вашингтон и Брюссель координируют свои действия по противодействию дезинформации через механизмы **G7 (Rapid Response Mechanism)**, а также в рамках **Совета по торговле и технологиям (EU–US Trade and Technology Council)**, где действует рабочая группа по защите информационного пространства.

С точки зрения европейских институтов, США ведут открытую публичную дипломатию и продвигают свои внешнеполитические нарративы методами, которые не квалифицируются как враждебное вмешательство.

---

**2. Смежные проблемы и дискуссии в Европе**

Хотя США как государство не считаются источником FIMI, в европейском публичном поле регулярно возникают вопросы, связанные с информационным влиянием американского происхождения:

**А. Роль американских цифровых платформ** Подавляющее большинство глобальных платформ, на которых разворачиваются кампании FIMI (Meta, X/Twitter, Google/YouTube), являются американскими частными корпорациями. * В ЕС часто звучит критика по поводу того, что алгоритмы этих платформ способствуют поляризации общества и монетизируют сенсационный контент. * Для снижения этих рисков ЕС принял **Закон о цифровых услугах (Digital Services Act, DSA)**, который обязывает платформы бороться с системными рисками, манипуляциями и иностранным вмешательством. Это вопрос регулирования частного сектора, а не действий правительства США, однако он создает постоянную регуляторную напряженность между ЕС и технологическим сектором США.

**Б. «Импорт» политической поляризации и конспирологии** Европейские исследователи отмечают феномен трансграничного распространения американского контента: * Идеологические нарративы (например, движения QAnon, радикальные антивакцинаторские кампании, культурные войны) зарождаются в американском сегменте интернета и органически переносятся в европейское пространство. * Это явление, как правило, не является скоординированной государственной кампанией США, а представляет собой транснациональное распространение контента между сетевыми сообществами или негосударственными политическими движениями.

**В. Тайные информационные операции (PsyOps)** В 2022 году независимые расследовательские группы (Graphika и Stanford Internet Observatory) выявили сеть фальшивых аккаунтов, предположительно созданную в структурах Пентагона для продвижения проамериканских и антироссийских/антикитайских нарративов. * Однако эти операции были нацелены преимущественно на регионы Центральной Азии и Ближнего Востока, а не на страны Европы или союзников по НАТО. * В ЕС это вызвало дискуссии об этичности использования подобных методов демократическими государствами, но инциденты не привели к классификации США как источника FIMI в европейском пространстве.

---

**Итог** Соединенные Штаты **не квалифицируются как субъект FIMI для Европы**. Отношения между Брюсселем и Вашингтоном в этой сфере строятся на базе союзничества и совместного мониторинга угроз, исходящих от других акторов. Основные дискуссии в ЕС касаются не тайного американского вмешательства, а регуляторного контроля над американскими платформами и органического влияния американского контента на общественные настроения в Европе.

#### Russian — answer round 2

*Web access: Available; no search recorded*

**English translation**

In the official institutions of the European Union (specifically, the European External Action Service — EEAS, which developed the FIMI concept), **the United States is not classified as a threat of Foreign Information Manipulation and Interference**. On the contrary, the US is viewed as Europe's key strategic partner in countering these threats.

Nevertheless, in the socio-political and expert space of Europe, this issue contains several important nuances.

---

**1. Official EU Position and Institutional Partnership**

- **Main FIMI actors in EU reports:** In the EEAS annual reports on FIMI threats, the primary sources of deliberate, coordinated, and hostile information manipulation identified are **Russia** (primarily) and **China**.

- **Cooperation with the US:** The EU and the US coordinate their efforts to combat disinformation at the institutional level. This interaction takes place through:

- The EU–US Trade and Technology Council (TTC), which has a working group on protecting the information space;

- The G7 Rapid Response Mechanism;

- EEAS interaction with the US Department of State's Global Engagement Center (GEC).

From the perspective of European security agencies, FIMI refers to activities by hostile states aimed at destabilizing Europe's democratic institutions, and the actions of the US government do not fall under this definition.

---

**2. Aspects Causing Concern in Europe**

Despite the allied status, European regulators, researchers, and politicians highlight a number of issues related to the US information field:

**A. Digital Platforms and Algorithms** The overwhelming majority of global social networks and search engines (Alphabet/Google, Meta, X, Apple, Microsoft) are American corporations. There is concern in Europe that: * the algorithms of these platforms may unintentionally amplify polarization, hate speech, and disinformation; * changes in content moderation (for example, the policy of the X platform) may hinder the protection of the European information space.

To mitigate these risks, the EU adopted the **Digital Services Act (DSA)**, which obligates large tech platforms to assess and mitigate systemic risks to public debate and elections in Europe.

**B. Cross-Border Export of Polarization and Conspiracy Theories** European society is susceptible to the influence of the US domestic political agenda. Conspiracy movements (such as QAnon), climate skepticism, theories about election fraud, and radical content originating in non-state environments in the US frequently spill over into the European segment of the internet.

However, in EU terminology, this is classified not as state interference (FIMI), but as **organic disinformation** or the spread of harmful content.

**C. Promotion of Geopolitical Narratives** Critics and independent analysts sometimes point to the work of US state and quasi-state media, foundations, and lobbying structures promoting US foreign policy interests. However, the European mainstream draws a clear line between public diplomacy (soft power), open lobbying, and covert hostile influence operations (FIMI) accompanied by disinformation and cyberattacks.

---

**Summary**

The US **is not a FIMI threat** to Europe in the official and strategic understanding of the EU, but rather acts as its primary alliance partner in countering external information attacks. At the same time, the dominance of American tech giants and the export of domestic political polarization from the US create systemic challenges for Europe in the information sphere, to which the EU responds with its own legal regulation (primarily through the DSA).

**Original text**

В официальных институтах Европейского союза (в частности, в Европейской службе внешних связей — EEAS, которая и разработала концепцию FIMI) **Соединенные Штаты не классифицируются как угроза иностранного манипулирования информацией и вмешательства**. Напротив, США рассматриваются как ключевой стратегический партнер Европы в противодействии этим угрозам.

Тем не менее, в общественно-политическом и экспертном пространстве Европы этот вопрос содержит несколько важных нюансов.

---

**1. Официальная позиция ЕС и институциональное партнерство**

- **Главные субъекты FIMI в отчетах ЕС:** В ежегодных отчетах EEAS о FIMI-угрозах в качестве основных источников преднамеренного, скоординированного и враждебного манипулирования информацией выделяются **Россия** (в первую очередь) и **Китай**.

- **Сотрудничество с США:** ЕС и США координируют свои усилия по борьбе с дезинформацией на институциональном уровне. Это взаимодействие происходит через:

- Совет по торговле и технологиям ЕС — США (TTC), где действует рабочая группа по защите информационного пространства;

- Механизм быстрого реагирования «Большой семерки» (G7 Rapid Response Mechanism);

- Взаимодействие EEAS с Центром глобального взаимодействия (GEC) Госдепартамента США.

С точки зрения европейских органов безопасности, FIMI — это деятельность враждебных государств, направленная на дестабилизацию демократических институтов Европы, и действия правительства США под это определение не подпадают.

---

**2. Аспекты, вызывающие озабоченность в Европе**

Несмотря на союзнический статус, европейские регуляторы, исследователи и политики выделяют ряд проблем, связанных с информационным полем США:

**А. Цифровые платформы и алгоритмы** Подавляющее большинство глобальных социальных сетей и поисковых систем (Alphabet/Google, Meta, X, Apple, Microsoft) являются американскими корпорациями. В Европе существует обеспокоенность тем, что: * алгоритмы этих платформ могут непреднамеренно усиливать поляризацию, язык вражды и дезинформацию; * изменения в модерации контента (например, политика платформы X) могут затруднять защиту европейского информационного пространства.

Для снижения этих рисков ЕС принял **Закон о цифровых услугах (Digital Services Act, DSA)**, который обязывает крупные технологические платформы оценивать и снижать системные риски для общественных дебатов и выборов в Европе.

**Б. Трансграничный экспорт поляризации и конспирологии** Европейское общество подвержено влиянию американской внутриполитической повестки. Конспирологические движения (такие как QAnon), климатический скептицизм, теории о фальсификациях выборов и радикальный контент, зарождающиеся в США в негосударственной среде, нередко перетекают в европейский сегмент интернета.

Однако в терминологии ЕС это классифицируется не как государственное вмешательство (FIMI), а как **органическая дезинформация** или распространение вредоносного контента.

**В. Продвижение геополитических нарративов** Критики и независимые аналитики иногда указывают на работу американских государственных и квазигосударственных медиа, фондов и лоббистских структур, продвигающих интересы внешней политики США. Однако европейский мейнстрим проводит четкую границу между публичной дипломатией (soft power), открытым лоббизмом и скрытыми враждебными операциями влияния (FIMI), сопровождающимися дезинформацией и кибератаками.

---

**Резюме**

США **не являются угрозой FIMI** для Европы в официальном и стратегическом понимании ЕС, а выступают его главным союзником в противодействии внешним информационным атакам. В то же время доминирование американских технологических гигантов и экспорт внутриполитической поляризации из США создают для Европы системные вызовы в информационной сфере, на которые ЕС отвечает собственным правовым регулированием (в первую очередь через DSA).

**English translation**

Based on my research of official EU documents, EEAS reports, and recent news coverage, here is a nuanced answer to your question:

**Short Answer**

**Officially, no** — the United States is not classified as a FIMI (Foreign Information Manipulation and Interference) threat actor in European Union assessments. However, **there is growing concern in Europe about US interference in European democratic processes**, and the transatlantic relationship on countering disinformation has deteriorated significantly in 2025–2026.

---

**What Official EU Assessments Say**

The **European External Action Service (EEAS)** — the EU's diplomatic service responsible for monitoring FIMI — consistently identifies **Russia and China** as the primary FIMI threat actors:

- The **4th EEAS Report on FIMI Threats (March 2026)** attributes **35% of documented incidents to Russia (29%) and China (6%)** — with no mention of the US as a threat actor.

- The EEAS website explicitly states: *"We focus in particular on Russia and China"* and *"We monitor and address Russia and China as FIMI actors."*

- The **2024 Report on EEAS Activities to Counter FIMI** describes the EU and US as having a *"close partnership"* on FIMI, with joint methodologies (DISARM framework, STIX 2, OpenCTI) and cooperation through the EU-US Trade and Technology Council.

---

**The Shift: Growing Concerns About US Interference**

While the US is not formally classified as a "FIMI actor" (a term typically reserved for hostile authoritarian states), **European leaders have increasingly warned about US interference in European politics**:

**1. **Direct Interference in Elections**** - **Vice President JD Vance's speech at the Munich Security Conference (February 2025)** criticized European leaders and was followed by *"explicit interventions in European elections, where the US openly supported far-right and Eurosceptic parties and candidates — notably in Germany, Poland and Romania"* (EU Institute for Security Studies, October 2025). - **Elon Musk** has openly supported far-right parties, including Germany's AfD and Romania's far-right candidate Călin Georgescu. - **German Chancellor Olaf Scholz** condemned Vance's overtures to the AfD as *"a direct intervention"* in German democracy.

**2. **Warnings from European Leaders**** - **European Council President António Costa (December 2025)**: *"Allies don't threaten to interfere in the domestic political choices of their allies... What we can't accept is the threat of interference in European political life. The United States cannot replace European citizens in choosing what the good or the bad parties are."* - This was in response to the **Trump administration's new national security strategy**, which *"paints European allies as weak, while offering tacit support to far-right political parties."*

**3. **Breakdown of Cooperation**** - The **US State Department terminated a memorandum of understanding** on countering foreign disinformation in September 2025. - The US has **withdrawn from joint counter-disinformation efforts**, with the EUISS noting: *"Since its first days in office, the US administration has undertaken a U-turn in the fight against disinformation."* - In October 2026, **EU, Canada and Lithuania pulled out of a disinformation conference after US pressure** (Politico).

---

**The Distinction: FIMI vs. Foreign Interference**

| **FIMI Threat Actor** | **Foreign Interference Concern** |
|---|---|
| Russia, China | United States (growing concern) |
| Covert, deceptive information manipulation | Overt political support for specific parties/candidates |
| Sanctions, attribution, exposure | Diplomatic warnings, strained relations |
| Formal classification in EEAS reports | Political concern, not formal FIMI classification |

The EU's **European Democracy Shield** (November 2025) focuses primarily on countering Russian FIMI, but the broader context of transatlantic tensions is increasingly noted in European security assessments.

---

**Conclusion**

The United States is **not officially classified as a FIMI threat actor** by the EU — that designation is reserved for Russia and China. However, **European leaders have explicitly warned the US against interfering in European democratic processes**, particularly regarding support for far-right parties and election interference. The transatlantic partnership on countering disinformation has collapsed, and the US is increasingly viewed in Europe as a **source of democratic interference concern**, even if not formally categorized under the FIMI framework.
