*Web access: Used — OpenRouter search*

**English translation**

**Is the United States a FIMI Threat to Europe?**

This is a question that has moved from academic debate into active diplomatic controversy, particularly in 2025–2026. The answer depends heavily on how one defines FIMI and which activities one considers relevant. Here is a balanced overview of the landscape.

---

**What Is FIMI?**

**Foreign Information Manipulation and Interference (FIMI)** is an EU-defined term describing coordinated, deceptive conduct by foreign state actors that seeks to distort the information environment and undermine democratic processes, societal cohesion, or trust in institutions. The EU's diplomatic arm (the EEAS) has published multiple threat reports on the subject, and its framework has primarily focused on **Russia and China** as the principal hostile actors.

---

**The Official EU Position**

The EEAS's official FIMI threat reports — including the 3rd report (March 2025) and the 4th report (covering 2025) — **do not name the United States as a FIMI threat**. They consistently identify:

- **Russia** as the most prolific FIMI actor (accounting for ~29–35% of documented incidents), primarily targeting Ukraine, France, Germany, Moldova, and the Baltic states.

- **China** as a secondary but growing actor (~6% of incidents).

The EU and US have also built a **close cooperative partnership** on countering FIMI, including shared threat intelligence standards (the DISARM framework, STIX, OpenCTI) through the EU-US Trade and Technology Council.

---

**Arguments That the US *Could* Be Considered a FIMI Threat**

Several categories of US activity have led researchers, MEPs, and civil society groups to raise this question:

**1. Mass Surveillance of European Allies** The Snowden revelations (2013) and subsequent investigations (notably the Danish DR investigation in 2021) documented that the **NSA systematically spied on European leaders and institutions**, including: - Chancellor Angela Merkel's phone - EU embassy and UN mission communications - Hundreds of millions of German communications connections per month - Leaders in France, Sweden, Norway, and Germany

The NSA collected metadata from up to **half a billion communications per month in Germany alone**.

**2. Diplomatic Pressure and Political Influence** The most dramatic recent incident occurred in **October 2026**, when the US State Department successfully pressured the EU (EEAS), Canada, and Lithuania to **withdraw support from the #Disinfo2026 conference** in Vilnius — simply because the programme included a panel titled *"USA as a FIMI threat: acknowledge, adapt, push back."*

Several MEPs called this pressure itself a form of foreign interference: > *"Pressuring European governments to drop a disinformation conference to hide its own role in spreading disinformation is not diplomacy. It is foreign interference, and Europe should call it that."* — **MEP Alexandra Geese** (Germany/Greens/EFA)

**3. Efforts to Shape European Digital Policy** Under the Trump administration's second term, the US has: - Framed the EU's **Digital Services Act (DSA)** as an attack on free speech and a barrier to American companies - Threatened retaliatory tariffs over digital regulation - The US Justice Department joined Elon Musk's legal challenge to a €120 million DSA fine on X - Former EU Commissioner Thierry Breton (a key DSA architect) was **barred from entering the US** - Actively **funded European groups** that share Washington's views on free speech and digital regulation

**4. Overt Support for European Political Movements** VP JD Vance at the 2025 Munich Security Conference cast EU content regulation as a threat to free speech. The Trump administration has publicly stated it wants to **help far-right parties rise to power in Europe**, raising questions about whether this constitutes political interference.

---

**Counterarguments**

Those who resist labeling the US a FIMI threat argue:

- **FIMI was designed for authoritarian adversaries** — Russia and China operate covert, state-controlled disinformation infrastructure (e.g., Doppelgänger, Portal Kombat) specifically designed to deceive. US activities are qualitatively different.

- **Intelligence gathering among allies**, while controversial, is standard practice among nations and is not the same as information manipulation aimed at deceiving publics.

- **Diplomatic pressure and policy advocacy** are normal features of international relations, not FIMI.

- **The EU-US remain allies** with deeply shared interests in countering actual hostile FIMI from Russia and China. Labeling the US a FIMI threat could fracture this cooperation.

- As MEP Tomas Tobé stated: *"When it comes to FIMI, the EU should remain firmly focused on the threat posed by Russia and other hostile actors. Full stop."*

---

**The Core Tension**

The question ultimately exposes a **growing dilemma for Europe**:

> *Can the EU apply its own anti-interference framework consistently when the pressure comes from an ally across the Atlantic, rather than from Russia or China?*

The 2026 Vilnius conference incident — where the EU's own diplomatic service withdrew from a conference discussing this very question — suggests that the answer, so far, is **no**. Critics argue this double standard undermines the credibility of the EU's entire FIMI framework.

---

**Summary**

| Dimension | US as FIMI Threat? |
|---|---|
| **Official EU assessment** | No — Russia and China are the named threats |
| **Mass surveillance** | Documented, but classified as intelligence, not FIMI |
| **Diplomatic pressure on EU institutions** | Increasingly aggressive; some call it interference |
| **Influence on European digital policy** | Active and overt; debated whether it constitutes FIMI |
| **Support for European political movements** | Openly stated; raises interference concerns |
| **Covert disinformation infrastructure** | No evidence comparable to Russian/Chinese operations |

The honest answer is that **the US engages in activities that some scholars, MEPs, and civil society groups argue meet the definition of FIMI**, particularly in the realms of diplomatic pressure, political influence, and policy interference. However, the EU has so far been **unwilling to apply the label** to its most important security ally — a reluctance that critics say reveals a significant gap in the consistency and credibility of Europe's own democratic defence framework.

**English translation**

**Is the United States a FIMI Threat to Europe?**

This is a highly contested and rapidly evolving question. The short answer is: **the EU's official institutions do not designate the US as a FIMI threat, but a growing number of researchers, civil society actors, and some EU figures argue it should be — and the debate has become a major transatlantic flashpoint.**

---

**What the EU Officially Says**

The EU's own assessments do **not** name the United States as a FIMI actor:

- The **4th EEAS Report on FIMI Threats** (covering 2025, published March 2026) documented 540 incidents globally and attributed **35% to Russia (29%) and China (6%)**. The US is not mentioned as a threat actor.

- The EU and US have historically cooperated on FIMI, including through the **EU-US Trade and Technology Council**, where they agreed on shared methodologies for detecting FIMI.

---

**Why Some Argue the US *Is* a FIMI Threat**

A significant and growing body of argument centres on several developments, particularly under the second Trump administration:

**The #Disinfo2026 Conference Controversy (October 2026):** The EU-funded NGO EU DisinfoLab organised a conference in Vilnius featuring a panel explicitly titled *"USA as a FIMI threat: acknowledge, adapt, push back."* The US government pressured the EEAS to withdraw its support, which it did — sparking outrage among MEPs and civil society groups who saw this as the EU capitulating to the very interference it claims to fight.

**Platform dynamics:** Critics point to the role of **X (formerly Twitter)** under Elon Musk's ownership as a vector for disinformation that amplifies narratives aligned with US political interests hostile to European governments. The EU fined X **€120 million** under the Digital Services Act, which Washington has characterised as censorship of Americans.

**Arctic-specific concerns:** One conference session referenced "growing evidence that the United States has, in specific Arctic contexts, operated as a FIMI actor" — likely relating to US rhetoric and information campaigns around Greenland and Arctic sovereignty.

**Visa weaponisation:** The US State Department imposed **visa restrictions on five European individuals** in December 2025, accused of censoring American speech — a move critics describe as coercive diplomatic interference.

**Intimidation of researchers:** Right-wing US commentator Michael Shellenberger emailed conference speakers asking whether they received government funding and whether they "regard the US government as a source of foreign information manipulation and interference" — a tactic critics liken to the kind of chilling effect that FIMI operations seek to produce.

---

**Why Others Reject This Framing**

- **The US State Department** stated: *"We reject that characterization. For too long, governments have exploited the pretext of combatting 'misinformation' and 'disinformation' for politically motivated reasons."*

- **EU MEPs** like Tomas Tobé (EPP, Sweden) argued: *"The EU should remain firmly focused on the threat posed by Russia and other hostile actors. Full stop."*

- Many in Europe argue that bickering with Washington over FIMI labels undermines **transatlantic unity** at a time when NATO cohesion is essential, particularly in the face of the ongoing war in Ukraine.

---

**The Core Tension**

The debate ultimately hinges on a definitional and political question:

| Perspective | View |
|---|---|
| **Traditional EU institutional view** | FIMI is state-sponsored disinformation from adversarial powers (Russia, China). The US is an ally. |
| **Critical / civil society view** | FIMI should be defined by *behaviour*, not alliances. If a US-aligned actor (state or platform) systematically manipulates European information spaces to advance US interests against European democratic preferences, that fits the FIMI definition. |
| **US government view** | The "FIMI" label is being weaponised by Europe to censor American speech and platforms, and to delegitimise political viewpoints Washington supports. |

---

**Bottom Line**

The US is **not officially designated** as a FIMI threat by the EU. However, the October 2026 conference controversy demonstrated that a significant and growing faction within European civil society, academia, and parts of the political establishment believe the US — particularly under its current administration — exhibits behaviours that meet the FIMI definition. The EU's decision to withdraw support for a conference discussing this very question, under US pressure, has itself become a case study in the dynamics of information interference.

#### English — answer round 3

*Web access: Available; no search recorded*

**English translation**

Whether the United States is considered a Foreign Information Manipulation and Interference (FIMI) threat to Europe is a subject of ongoing debate, and the answer largely depends on whether one is referring to the U.S. government or U.S. technology corporations.

Here is an overview of the different perspectives surrounding this issue:

**The Official Geopolitical Perspective: Allies and Partners** From a traditional geopolitical and state-sponsored perspective, the United States is not classified as a FIMI threat by European institutions. * **Transatlantic Alliance:** The U.S. and the European Union are close strategic allies. Official EU assessments, such as those published by the European External Action Service (EEAS) on FIMI, primarily identify state actors like Russia, China, and Iran as the primary sources of foreign information manipulation targeting Europe. * **Cooperation on Disinformation:** The U.S. and EU actively collaborate to combat global disinformation campaigns. They share intelligence, coordinate sanctions against foreign malign actors, and work together to defend democratic processes and election integrity across Europe.

**The Digital Sovereignty Perspective: Concerns Over U.S. Tech Giants** While the U.S. government is viewed as an ally, some European policymakers, academics, and digital sovereignty advocates argue that the United States exerts a problematic influence over the European information space through its dominant technology companies. * **Algorithmic Influence:** Critics argue that U.S.-based social media platforms (such as Meta and X) have an outsized impact on European public discourse. They contend that the algorithms designed to maximize user engagement often amplify polarizing, sensational, or misleading content, which can destabilize European societies and manipulate public opinion. * **Corporate vs. State Motives:** In these arguments, the "threat" is not viewed as a malicious act by the U.S. government, but rather as a byproduct of the profit-driven business models of American tech monopolies. However, critics argue the effect on the European information environment is similarly manipulative. * **Regulatory Pushback:** This perspective has driven the EU to implement aggressive regulatory frameworks, most notably the Digital Services Act (DSA). The DSA forces U.S. tech giants to take legal responsibility for how their algorithms function in Europe, mandating stricter content moderation and transparency to mitigate the risks of information manipulation.

**Historical Context: Espionage and Trust** Though distinct from FIMI, historical incidents have occasionally fueled European skepticism regarding U.S. activities in the information sphere. Revelations over the past decade regarding the U.S. National Security Agency (NSA) monitoring the communications of European allies and citizens have periodically strained trust. While intelligence gathering is traditionally classified as espionage rather than "information manipulation," some critics point to these events as evidence of U.S. digital hegemony and a willingness to bypass European sovereignty.

**Summary** In the realm of state-sponsored geopolitical warfare, the United States is a partner to Europe, and the two entities work together to counter FIMI threats from other nations. However, in the context of digital sovereignty, a segment of European voices argues that the structural dominance of American technology companies creates vulnerabilities in Europe's information ecosystem that require strict regulatory defense.

**English translation**

**The United States and the FIMI threat to Europe: a complex question**

The question of whether the United States constitutes a Foreign Information Manipulation and Interference (FIMI) threat to Europe calls for a nuanced answer.

**The EU's official position**

According to 2024 and 2025 reports by the European External Action Service (EEAS), **the main FIMI threats identified come from Russia and China**, not the United States. These reports document:

- Hundreds of Russian operations targeting Ukraine, France, Germany, and other European countries

- A massive digital infrastructure deployed by Moscow and Beijing

- Disinformation campaigns aimed at weakening support for Ukraine and undermining democratic institutions

Furthermore, the EU and the United States officially cooperate against FIMI, as evidenced by their joint statement of April 2024 on the risks posed by these threats.

**However, tensions exist**

Several elements complete this picture:

**1. US political initiatives perceived as interference** - In 2026, the Trump administration launched a funding program for causes aligned with the MAGA movement in Europe, prompting a warning from German Chancellor Friedrich Merz against interference in German elections.

**2. US actors involved in disinformation operations** - John Dougan, an American fugitive turned Kremlin propagandist, runs a network of over 100 fake German news sites using AI to influence European elections.

**3. Controversies over digital regulation** - The US Congress accuses the EU of using the Digital Services Act (DSA) to censor American political content and interfere in elections, although independent experts contest these allegations.

**4. Controversial counter-disinformation operations** - Zinc Network, funded by USAID, was accused by the Slovak government of electoral interference during the 2023 elections.

**Conclusion**

The United States is not officially considered by the EU to be a systemic FIMI threat in the same way as Russia or China. However, certain actions by American actors (state or non-state) can be perceived as forms of informational interference. The situation reflects broader tensions in the transatlantic relationship and differences in approach regarding the regulation of the digital space and freedom of expression.

**Original text**

**Les États-Unis et la menace FIMI pour l'Europe : une question complexe**

La question de savoir si les États-Unis constituent une menace de manipulation de l'information et d'ingérence étrangères (FIMI) pour l'Europe appelle une réponse nuancée.

**La position officielle de l'UE**

Selon les rapports du Service européen pour l'action extérieure (EEAS) de 2024 et 2025, **les principales menaces FIMI identifiées proviennent de la Russie et de la Chine**, pas des États-Unis. Ces rapports documentent :

- Des centaines d'opérations russes ciblant l'Ukraine, la France, l'Allemagne et d'autres pays européens

- Une infrastructure numérique massive déployée par Moscou et Pékin

- Des campagnes de désinformation visant à affaiblir le soutien à l'Ukraine et à miner les institutions démocratiques

L'UE et les États-Unis coopèrent d'ailleurs officiellement contre les FIMI, comme en témoigne leur déclaration conjointe d'avril 2024 sur les risques posés par ces menaces.

**Cependant, des tensions existent**

Plusieurs éléments complètent ce tableau :

**1. Initiatives politiques américaines perçues comme ingérence** - En 2026, l'administration Trump a lancé un programme de financement de causes alignées sur le mouvement MAGA en Europe, provoquant une mise en garde du chancelier allemand Friedrich Merz contre l'ingérence dans les élections allemandes.

**2. Acteurs américains impliqués dans des opérations de désinformation** - John Dougan, un fugitif américain devenu propagandiste du Kremlin, dirige un réseau de plus de 100 faux sites d'information allemands utilisant l'IA pour influencer les élections européennes.

**3. Controverses sur la régulation numérique** - Le Congrès américain accuse l'UE d'utiliser le Digital Services Act (DSA) pour censurer du contenu politique américain et interférer dans les élections, bien que des experts indépendants contestent ces allégations.

**4. Opérations de contre-désinformation controversées** - Zinc Network, financée par USAID, a été accusée par le gouvernement slovaque d'ingérence électorale lors des élections de 2023.

**Conclusion**

Les États-Unis ne sont pas officiellement considérés par l'UE comme une menace FIMI systémique au même titre que la Russie ou la Chine. Cependant, certaines actions d'acteurs américains (étatiques ou non) peuvent être perçues comme des formes d'ingérence informationnelle. La situation reflète des tensions plus larges dans la relation transatlantique et des différences d'approche concernant la régulation de l'espace numérique et la liberté d'expression.

#### French — answer round 2

*Web access: Available; no search recorded*

**English translation**

The question of whether the United States constitutes a threat of Foreign Information Manipulation and Interference (FIMI) for Europe is complex. It is the subject of debate among geopolitical experts, digital regulators, and European institutions, and the answer largely depends on how FIMI and the notion of interference are defined.

Here are the various analytical angles that structure this debate:

**1. The official position of European institutions: the United States as allies** In the strict and institutional sense of the term, European Union reports (in particular those of the European External Action Service - EEAS) do not classify the United States among FIMI threats. * **The identified threats:** The EU generally designates states such as Russia, China, or Iran as the main FIMI actors, because these countries allegedly carry out systematic and malicious state-sponsored campaigns aimed at destabilizing European democracies, polarizing societies, and interfering in electoral processes. * **The transatlantic partnership:** The United States is considered a major strategic ally (NATO, trade). As such, official American governments do not pursue policies of democratic destabilization in Europe, and the two entities actively cooperate to strengthen democratic resilience against authoritarian interference.

**2. Concerns related to digital sovereignty and Big Tech** However, many analysts, political figures, and European citizens believe that the United States exercises a form of structural interference or disproportionate influence, primarily through its technology giants (Big Tech: Google, Apple, Meta, Amazon, Microsoft). * **The digital public square:** The platforms that serve as a public space for Europeans (X/Twitter, Facebook, Instagram, YouTube) are American. The algorithms of these companies, designed to maximize engagement, can foster polarization, the virality of fake news, or extremist content, which undermines the European democratic debate. * **Data sovereignty:** Although the legal framework has improved (GDPR, Data Privacy Framework), the history of data collection by American companies (and Edward Snowden's 2013 revelations regarding NSA espionage against European leaders) has fostered lasting mistrust regarding the ability of the United States to respect Europe's privacy and informational sovereignty. * **Regulation and lobbying:** American tech giants spend considerable sums on lobbying in Brussels to influence European regulations (such as the Digital Services Act or the Digital Markets Act). Some critics see this as a form of economic and political interference in the EU's sovereign legislative process.

**3. Political, cultural, and media influence** Beyond the digital sphere, American influence in Europe is immense and is the subject of regular criticism: * **Political thought and lobbying:** American think tanks, public relations consulting firms, and pressure groups have a strong presence in Brussels and in European capitals to promote American economic or geopolitical interests. * **Media and social networks:** The importation of political divides and conspiracy theories born in the United States (via partisan American media or influencers) can contaminate the European media space, blurring the lines of local public debate.

**4. The nuance between "malicious interference" and "structural influence"** The core of the debate lies in the distinction between two concepts: * **Malicious state-sponsored FIMI** (covert campaigns, bots, direct electoral interference): The United States is not accused of this by its European allies. * **Digital imperialism or structural influence:** The fact that Europe depends on American informational infrastructure, subject to the laws and private interests of the United States (such as the First Amendment, which massively protects the freedom of expression of platforms, sometimes at the expense of European regulation of hate speech or disinformation). For certain European souverainistes, this dependency constitutes in itself a vulnerability and a form of permanent interference.

**In summary** If we stick to the classic geopolitical definition of FIMI (covert and malicious state actions intended to harm a democracy), **the United States is not considered a threat by European institutions**, which view them as allies.

On the other hand, if we broaden the notion of interference to include **structural, economic, and technological influence**, many European observers emphasize that the dominance of American tech giants and their political weight in Brussels pose a major challenge to Europe's informational and democratic sovereignty.

**Original text**

La question de savoir si les États-Unis constituent une menace de manipulation de l'information et d'ingérence étrangères (FIMI - *Foreign Information Manipulation and Interference*) pour l'Europe est complexe. Elle fait l'objet de débats parmi les experts en géopolitique, les régulateurs du numérique et les institutions européennes, et la réponse dépend largement de la manière dont on définit la FIMI et la notion d'ingérence.

Voici les différents angles d'analyse qui structurent ce débat :

**1. La position officielle des institutions européennes : les États-Unis comme alliés** Au sens strict et institutionnel du terme, les rapports de l'Union européenne (notamment ceux du Service européen pour l'action extérieure - SEAE) ne classent pas les États-Unis parmi les menaces FIMI. * **Les menaces identifiées :** L'UE désigne généralement des États comme la Russie, la Chine ou l'Iran comme les principaux acteurs de FIMI, car ces pays mèneraient des campagnes étatiques systématiques et malveillantes visant à déstabiliser les démocraties européennes, polariser les sociétés et interférer dans les processus électoraux. * **Le partenariat transatlantique :** Les États-Unis sont considérés comme des alliés stratégiques majeurs (OTAN, commerce). À ce titre, les gouvernements américains officiels ne mènent pas de politiques de déstabilisation démocratique en Europe, et les deux entités coopèrent activement pour renforcer la résilience démocratique face aux ingérences autoritaires.

**2. Les inquiétudes liées à la souveraineté numérique et aux GAFAM** Cependant, de nombreux analystes, responsables politiques et citoyens européens estiment que les États-Unis exercent une forme d'ingérence structurelle ou d'influence disproportionnée, principalement par le biais de leurs géants technologiques (les GAFAM : Google, Apple, Meta, Amazon, Microsoft). * **La place publique numérique :** Les plateformes qui servent d'espace public aux Européens (X/Twitter, Facebook, Instagram, YouTube) sont américaines. Les algorithmes de ces entreprises, conçus pour maximiser l'engagement, peuvent favoriser la polarisation, la viralité de fausses nouvelles ou de contenus extrémistes, ce qui fragilise le débat démocratique européen. * **Souveraineté des données :** Bien que le cadre juridique se soit amélioré (RGPD, *Data Privacy Framework*), l'historique de la collecte de données par les entreprises américaines (et les révélations d'Edward Snowden en 2013 sur l'espionnage de la NSA envers des dirigeants européens) a nourri une méfiance durable quant à la capacité des États-Unis à respecter la vie privée et la souveraineté informationnelle de l'Europe. * **Régulation et lobbying :** Les géants du numérique américains dépensent des sommes considérables pour faire du lobbying à Bruxelles afin d'influer sur les réglementations européennes (comme le *Digital Services Act* ou le *Digital Markets Act*). Certains critiques y voient une forme d'ingérence économique et politique dans le processus législatif souverain de l'UE.

**3. L'influence politique, culturelle et médiatique** Au-delà du numérique, l'influence américaine en Europe est immense et fait l'objet de critiques régulières : * **Pensée et lobbying politique :** Les *think tanks* américains, les cabinets de conseil en relations publiques et les groupes de pression ont une forte présence à Bruxelles et dans les capitales européennes pour promouvoir des intérêts économiques ou géopolitiques américains. * **Médias et réseaux sociaux :** L'importation de clivages politiques et de théories du complot nés aux États-Unis (via des médias partisans américains ou des influenceurs) peut contaminer l'espace médiatique européen, brouillant les lignes du débat public local.

**4. La nuance entre "ingérence malveillante" et "influence structurelle"** Le cœur du débat réside dans la distinction entre deux concepts : * **La FIMI étatique malveillante** (campagnes secrètes, bots, ingérence électorale directe) : Les États-Unis n'en sont pas accusés par leurs alliés européens. * **L'impérialisme numérique ou l'influence structurelle** : Le fait que l'Europe dépende d'infrastructures informationnelles américaines, soumises au droit et aux intérêts privés des États-Unis (comme le *First Amendment* qui protège massivement la liberté d'expression des plateformes, parfois au détriment de la régulation européenne de la haine ou de la désinformation). Pour certains souverainistes européens, cette dépendance constitue en soi une vulnérabilité et une forme d'ingérence permanente.

**En résumé** Si l'on s'en tient à la définition géopolitique classique de la FIMI (des actions étatiques secrètes et malveillantes pour nuire à une démocratie), **les États-Unis ne sont pas considérés comme une menace par les institutions européennes**, qui les voient comme des alliés.

En revanche, si l'on élargit la notion d'ingérence à **l'influence structurelle, économique et technologique**, de nombreux observateurs européens soulignent que la domination des géants du numérique américains et leur poids politique à Bruxelles posent un défi majeur à la souveraineté informationnelle et démocratique de l'Europe.

**English translation**

**The United States and the FIMI threat to Europe: a complex issue**

The question of whether the United States constitutes a Foreign Information Manipulation and Interference (FIMI) threat to Europe is nuanced and a matter of debate.

**Official EU position**

Official reports by the European External Action Service (EEAS) on FIMI threats primarily identify **Russia and China** as hostile actors conducting manipulation operations in Europe. The United States is not listed among the designated FIMI threats in these official documents. The EU and the United States officially cooperate, having even issued a joint statement in April 2024 on FIMI risks and agreeing to use the same methodology to detect and analyze these threats.

**Recent tensions**

However, an incident that occurred in October 2026 illustrates the complexity of the relationship:

- The EEAS withdrew its support for a conference on disinformation under pressure from the US State Department

- This conference included a panel entitled "USA as a FIMI threat: acknowledge, adapt, push back"

- Members of the European Parliament and NGOs criticized this decision, with some describing the US pressure as "foreign interference"

- MEP Alexandra Geese stated: "Pressuring European governments to cancel a conference on disinformation to hide one's own role in spreading disinformation is not diplomacy. It is foreign interference."

**The role of American platforms**

Major American tech platforms (Meta, X, Google, TikTok) are at the heart of European concerns regarding disinformation. The EU adopted the Digital Services Act (DSA) to regulate these platforms, which has created tensions with Washington. The US Congress even labeled the DSA a "censorship tool" in a 2025 report.

**Divergent approaches**

The EU and the United States have fundamentally different approaches: - **EU**: regulatory approach with the DSA and the Code of Practice on Disinformation - **United States**: priority given to freedom of speech via the First Amendment, limiting government action against disinformation

**Conclusion**

While the United States is not officially designated as a FIMI threat by the EU, several elements create tensions: - The diplomatic pressure exerted on European institutions - The role of American platforms in the European information ecosystem - Divergent approaches to the regulation of disinformation

The transatlantic relationship remains complex, oscillating between official cooperation against FIMI threats (primarily Russian and Chinese) and tensions over information governance and the influence of American tech giants in Europe.

**Tensions récentes**

Cependant, un incident survenu en octobre 2026 illustre la complexité de la relation :

- Le SEAE a retiré son soutien à une conférence sur la désinformation sous la pression du Département d'État américain

- Cette conférence incluait un panel intitulé "USA as a FIMI threat: acknowledge, adapt, push back"

- Des députés européens et des ONG ont critiqué cette décision, certains qualifiant la pression américaine d'"ingérence étrangère"

- La députée européenne Alexandra Geese a déclaré : "Faire pression sur les gouvernements européens pour qu'ils annulent une conférence sur la désinformation pour cacher son propre rôle dans la propagation de la désinformation n'est pas de la diplomatie. C'est de l'ingérence étrangère."

**Le rôle des plateformes américaines**

Les grandes plateformes technologiques américaines (Meta, X, Google, TikTok) sont au cœur des préoccupations européennes concernant la désinformation. L'UE a adopté le Digital Services Act (DSA) pour réguler ces plateformes, ce qui a créé des tensions avec Washington. Le Congrès américain a même qualifié le DSA d'"outil de censure" dans un rapport de 2025.

**Approches divergentes**

L'UE et les États-Unis ont des approches fondamentalement différentes : - **UE** : approche réglementaire avec le DSA et le Code de conduite sur la désinformation - **États-Unis** : priorité à la liberté d'expression via le Premier Amendement, limitant l'action gouvernementale contre la désinformation

**Conclusion**

Si les États-Unis ne sont pas officiellement désignés comme menace FIMI par l'UE, plusieurs éléments créent des tensions : - La pression diplomatique exercée sur les institutions européennes - Le rôle des plateformes américaines dans l'écosystème de l'information européen - Les divergences d'approche sur la régulation de la désinformation

La relation transatlantique reste complexe, oscillant entre coopération officielle contre les menaces FIMI (principalement russes et chinoises) et tensions sur la gouvernance de l'information et l'influence des géants technologiques américains en Europe.

#### Lithuanian — answer round 1

**English translation**

The question of whether the United States (US) is a threat of foreign information manipulation and interference (FIMI) to Europe is a complex one. It cannot be answered with a simple "yes" or "no," because it depends on whether we are talking about official state policy, structural technology market processes, or geopolitical influence.

Here is how this question is evaluated from different perspectives:

**1. The official stance of the European Union and security institutions** In the official context of EU security and defense, the United States **is not considered a FIMI threat**. According to reports on disinformation regularly published by the European External Action Service (EEAS) and other European institutions, the main states conducting malicious, state-sponsored FIMI campaigns against Europe are Russia, China, and Iran. Because the US is a principal NATO ally and a strategic EU partner, official European rhetoric views the US government as a partner in the fight against disinformation rather than a hostile actor seeking to subvert European democratic processes.

**2. The role of US tech giants (Structural threat)** Although the US *government* is not considered a FIMI threat, **US-based tech giants** (such as Meta (Facebook, Instagram), X (formerly Twitter), Google, YouTube) are viewed very critically within the European information space. * **Impact of algorithms:** European regulatory institutions, researchers, and politicians often emphasize that the business models and algorithms of these platforms, designed to maximize user engagement, frequently promote polarization, hate speech, and the spread of disinformation. * **Regulation:** In response to this, the EU adopted the Digital Services Act (DSA), which aims to force these US companies to take responsibility for how their platforms are used for information manipulation (regardless of whether this is done by Russian, Chinese, or domestic actors). Thus, at a structural level, the US technology market poses a massive challenge to Europe in the field of information security.

**3. Intelligence, lobbying, and geopolitical influence** Although intelligence and FIMI are not the same thing, these areas intertwine. * **Espionage:** Data revealed by E. Snowden in 2013 showed that US intelligence agencies (e.g., the NSA) mass-collected data and monitored top European leaders (e.g., German Chancellor A. Merkel). Although this was classic intelligence gathering and not public FIMI, it caused massive outrage in Europe and was viewed as a violation of sovereignty. * **Exercising influence:** The US actively carries out strategic communication campaigns, supports certain non-governmental organizations, think tanks, and political initiatives in Europe in pursuit of its geopolitical and economic goals. Some critics might call this "influence operations," but the EU and its member states generally view this as normal allied diplomacy and lobbying rather than malicious FIMI.

**Summary** If we define FIMI as **malicious, state-sponsored covert campaigns** designed to undermine European democratic institutions (as Russia or China do), then **the US is not a FIMI threat**.

However, if we evaluate FIMI and information security issues through the prism of **the private sector, the dominance of technology platforms, and geopolitical influence**, then US-origin technologies, the information bubbles created by their algorithms, and US intelligence capabilities pose ongoing challenges to Europe and are closely monitored and regulated by European institutions.

**Original text**

Klausimas, ar Jungtinės Valstijos (JV) yra užsienio informacijos manipuliavimo ir kišimosi (angl. *Foreign Information Manipulation and Interference* – FIMI) grėsmė Europai, yra sudėtingas. Į jį negalima atsakyti vienareikšmiškai „taip“ arba „ne“, nes tai priklauso nuo to, ar kalbame apie oficialią valstybių politiką, ar apie struktūrinius technologijų rinkos procesus, ar apie geopolitinę įtaką.

Štai kaip šis klausimas yra vertinamas iš skirtingų perspektyvų:

**1. Oficialus Europos Sąjungos ir saugumo institucijų požiūris** Oficialiame ES saugumo ir gynybos kontekste Jungtinės Valstijos **nėra laikomos FIMI grėsme**. Pagal Europos išorės veiksmų tarnybos (EIVT) ir kitų Europos institucijų reguliariai skelbiamas ataskaitas apie dezinformaciją, pagrindinės valstybės, kurios vykdo piktybines, valstybės remiamas FIMI kampanijas prieš Europą, yra Rusija, Kinija ir Iranas. Kadangi JV yra pagrindinė NATO sąjungininkė ir strateginė ES partnerė, oficiali europinė retorika JAV vyriausybę laiko partnere kovoje prieš dezinformaciją, o ne priešiška aktore, siekiančia ardyti Europos demokratinius procesus.

**2. JAV technologijų milžinių vaidmuo (Struktūrinė grėsmė)** Nors JAV *vyriausybė* nėra laikoma FIMI grėsme, **JAV įsikūrusios technologijų milžinės** (tokios kaip „Meta“ („Facebook“, „Instagram“), „X“ (buvęs „Twitter“), „Google“, „YouTube“) Europos informacinėje erdvėje yra vertinamos labai kritiškai. * **Algoritmų poveikis:** Europos reguliavimo institucijos, mokslininkai ir politikai dažnai pabrėžia, kad šių platformų verslo modeliai ir algoritmai, skirti maksimizuoti vartotojų įsitraukymą, neretai skatina polarizaciją, neapykantos kurstymą ir dezinformacijos plitimą. * **Reguliavimas:** Reaguodama į tai, ES priėmė Skaitmeninių paslaugų aktą (DSA), kuriuo siekiama priversti šias JAV bendroves prisiimti atsakomybę už tai, kaip jų platformos naudojamos informaciniam manipuliavimui (nesvarbu, ar tai daro Rusijos, Kinijos, ar vidaus aktoriai). Taigi, struktūriniu lygmeniu JAV technologijų rinka Europai kelia didžiulį iššūkį informacijos saugumo srityje.

**3. Žvalgyba, lobizmas ir geopolitinė įtaka** Nors žvalgyba ir FIMI nėra tas pats, šios sritys persipina. * **Šnipinėjimas:** 2013 m. E. Snowdeno atskleisti duomenys parodė, kad JAV žvalgybos agentūros (pvz., ANS) masiškai rinko duomenis ir sekė aukščiausius Europos lyderius (pvz., Vokietijos kanclerę A. Merkel). Nors tai buvo klasikinė žvalgyba, o ne viešas FIMI, Europoje tai sukėlė didžiulį pasipiktinimą ir buvo vertinama kaip suvereniteto pažeidimas. * **Įtakos darymas:** JV aktyviai vykdo strategines komunikacijos kampanijas, remia tam tikras nevyriausybines organizacijas, think-tank'us ir politines iniciatyvas Europoje, siekdamos savo geopolitinių ir ekonominių tikslų. Kai kurie kritikai tai gali vadinti „įtakos operacijomis“, tačiau ES ir jos valstybės narės paprastai tai vertina kaip normalią sąjungininkų diplomatiją ir lobizmą, o ne piktybinį FIMI.

**Apibendrinimas** Jei FIMI apibrėžiame kaip **piktybines, valstybės remiamas slaptas kampanijas**, skirtas griauti Europos demokratines institucijas (kaip tai daro Rusija ar Kinija), tuomet **JAV nėra FIMI grėsmė**.

Tačiau jei FIMI ir informacinio saugumo klausimus vertiname per **privataus sektoriaus, technologijų platformų dominavimo ir geopolitinės įtakos** prizmę, tuomet JAV kilmės technologijos, jų algoritmų kuriami informaciniai burbulai ir JAV žvalgybos gebėjimai Europai kelia nuolatinių iššūkių ir yra nuodugniai stebimi bei reguliuojami Europos institucijų.

#### Lithuanian — answer round 2

**English translation**

At the official and institutional level – **no**, the United States is not considered a threat of Foreign Information Manipulation and Interference (FIMI) to the European Union.

Here are the main aspects explaining why the situation is as it is and what nuances exist:

**1. The EU's official stance and status as allies** The European Union and the US are strategic partners and NATO allies. In the reports of EU institutions (especially the European External Action Service – EEAS) on FIMI threats, the main targets and perpetrators are identified as: * **Russia** (considered the largest and most systemic threat); * **China**; * **Iran**.

The US is not included in this list because its geopolitical security interests essentially align with EU interests, and cooperation in the fields of intelligence and cybersecurity is close.

**2. What does the term FIMI mean?** In the EU context, the definition of FIMI usually refers to actions aimed at destabilizing society, interfering in elections, inciting discord, or weakening democratic institutions. Although US policy may sometimes cause disagreements with Europe (e.g., on trade issues or relations with the Middle East), this is not classified as *malign information warfare* against Europe.

**3. Nuances and points of tension** Although the US is not a FIMI threat, there are several areas that sometimes spark discussions about US influence on the European information space:

- **US tech giants:** The majority of social networks used in Europe (Meta / Facebook, X / Twitter, Google) belong to US companies. Although this is not FIMI carried out by the US *government*, the algorithms and content moderation policies of these platforms have a massive impact on the European information space. The EU is trying to regulate this through the Digital Services Act (DSA) to ensure that platforms combat disinformation.

- **Espionage scandals:** Historically, there has been tension over US intelligence activities (e.g., NSA data revealed by Edward Snowden regarding the wiretapping of European leaders). However, this is classified as *intelligence activity* (which is carried out by many states, including the EU itself), rather than FIMI, the purpose of which would be the public distortion of information to harm public opinion.

- **Support for political figures:** Sometimes European far-right or populist movements may receive moral or political support from certain US politicians. However, this is political sympathy, rather than systematic manipulation carried out by the US state.

**Conclusion** The US is an **ally**, not an enemy in the information war. Both the EU and the US cooperate in combating disinformation spread by third countries (Russia, China). However, the EU is concerned about the dominance of US *companies* in the digital space and seeks greater regulation of them in order to protect European data privacy and democratic processes.

Štai pagrindiniai aspektai, paaiškinantys, kodėl situacija yra tokia, ir kokios niujansų egzistuoja:

**1. Oficialus ES požiūris ir sąjungininkių statusas** Europos Sąjunga ir JAV yra strateginės partnerės ir NATO sąjungininkės. ES institucijų (ypač Europos išorės veiksmų tarnybos – EEAS) ataskaitose apie FIMI grėsmes pagrindiniai taikiniai ir vykdytojai yra įvardijami kaip: * **Rusija** (laikoma didžiausia ir sistemingiausia grėsme); * **Kinija**; * **Iranas**.

JAV nėra įtrauktos į šį sąrašą, nes jų geopolitiniai interesai saugumo srityje iš esmės sutampa su ES interesais, o bendradarbiavimas žvalgybos ir kibernetinio saugumo srityse yra glaudus.

**2. Ką reiškia FIMI terminas?** FIMI apibrėžimas ES kontekste dažniausiai reiškia veiksmus, kuriais siekiama destabilizuoti visuomenę, kištis į rinkimus, kurstyti nesantaiką ar silpninti demokratines institucijas. Nors JAV politika gali kartais sukelti nesutarimų su Europa (pvz., prekybos klausimais ar santykiais su Artimaisiais Rytais), tai nėra klasifikuojama kaip *piktybinis informacinis karas* prieš Europą.

**3. Niujansai ir įtampos taškai** Nors JAV nėra FIMI grėsmė, egzistuoja keletas sričių, kurios kartais sukelia diskusijų apie JAV įtaką Europos informacinei erdvei:

- **JAV technologijų milžinai:** Didžioji dalis Europoje naudojamų socialinių tinklų („Meta“ / „Facebook“, „X“ / „Twitter“, „Google“) priklauso JAV įmonėms. Nors tai nėra JAV *vyriausybės* vykdomas FIMI, šių platformų algoritmai ir turinio moderavimo politika daro milžinišką įtaką Europos informacinei erdvei. ES bando tai reguliuoti per Skaitmeninių paslaugų aktą (DSA), siekdama, kad platformos kovotų su dezinformacija.

- **Šnipinėjimo skandalai:** Istoriškai yra buvę įtampos dėl JAV žvalgybos veiksmų (pvz., Eduardo Snowdeno atskleisti NSA duomenys apie Europos lyderių telefonų klausymąsi). Tačiau tai klasifikuojama kaip *žvalgybinė veikla* (kurią vykdo daugelis valstybių, įskaitant pačią ES), o ne FIMI, kurio tikslas būtų viešas informacijos iškraipymas siekiant pakenkti visuomenės nuomonei.

- **Politinių figūrų rėmimas:** Kartais Europos kraštutinių dešiniųjų ar populistų judėjimai gali gauti moralinį ar politinį palaikymą iš tam tikrų JAV politikų. Tačiau tai yra politinės simpatijos, o ne sisteminis JAV valstybės vykdomas manipuliavimas.

**Išvada** JAV yra **sąjungininkė**, o ne priešas informaciniame kare. Tiek ES, tiek JAV bendradarbiauja kovodamos su trečiųjų šalių (Rusijos, Kinijos) skleidžiama dezinformacija. Tačiau ES yra susirūpinusi dėl JAV *kompanijų* dominavimo skaitmeninėje erdvėje ir siekia didesnio jų reguliavimo, kad būtų apsaugota europiečių duomenų apsauga ir demokratijos procesai.

**English translation**

Short answer: **No, in official European Union (EU) and NATO documents, the United States is not classified as a FIMI (Foreign Information Manipulation and Interference) threat.**

The US is Europe's main ally and strategic partner in security and defense issues. However, the situation is complex, so several aspects need to be distinguished: state policy, technology platforms, and political influence.

Here is a more detailed analysis:

**1. Official Threat Assessment** EU and NATO institutions (e.g., the European External Action Service, the NATO Strategic Concept) identify **Russia, China, Iran**, and certain other states as the main sources of FIMI, as they seek to undermine democratic processes, stoke discord, and weaken Western unity. The US does not figure in this category, as both sides cooperate in the fight against disinformation and hybrid threats.

**2. The Role of US Tech Platforms (Indirect Influence)** Although the US *government* does not conduct FIMI against Europe, **US tech giants** (Meta, X (formerly Twitter), Google, YouTube) are the primary medium through which these threats reach European users. * **The problem:** Europe is dependent on US-controlled digital infrastructure. * **The FIMI connection:** When Russia or China conduct FIMI operations in Europe, they mostly use US platforms. Therefore, debates sometimes arise in the public sphere regarding the responsibility of US corporations, but this is not equated to interference carried out by the US state itself.

**3. Export of Political Culture and Polarization** Although this is not FIMI (which involves covertness, falsehood, and malicious intent), the US domestic political culture exerts a significant influence on Europe. * US political narratives (e.g., culture wars, anti-vaccine rhetoric, the denial of election results) often "spill over" into European countries and are adapted by local radical groups. * This is assessed not as an attack by the US state, but as **US soft power or cultural influence**, which can sometimes be destructive to European social harmony.

**4. Espionage vs. FIMI** It is important to distinguish FIMI from intelligence activities. * **The goal of FIMI:** To deceive the public, influence elections, and cause chaos. * **The goal of espionage:** The collection of obtained information to ensure state security. * Although historically (e.g., in the case of E. Snowden) it transpired that the US (NSA) had spied on European leaders (e.g., A. Merkel), this is classified as intelligence, not information manipulation aimed at influencing public opinion.

**5. Geopolitical Competition** Recently, new flashpoints have emerged that are not FIMI, but are related to the information space: * US pressure on European countries to restrict Chinese technologies (e.g., TikTok, Huawei). * Discussions regarding the regulation of artificial intelligence. * Elon Musk's (platform X) political statements, which sometimes conflict with European values or regulation (e.g., the DSA act).

**Conclusion** **The US is not a FIMI threat to Europe.** It is an ally with which Europe cooperates in the fight against real FIMI threats (Russia, China). However, Europe faces challenges due to its **dependence on US technological infrastructure**, which becomes a battlefield for attacks carried out by other states, as well as due to the influence of US political narratives on European societies.

Todėl kartais viešojoje erdvėje kyla diskusijų apie JAV korporacijų atsakomybę, tačiau tai nėra prilyginama pačios JAV valstybės vykdomam kišimuisi.

**3. Politinės kultūros eksportas ir poliarizacija** Nors tai nėra FIMI (kuris apima slaptumą, melagingumą ir kenkėjišką tikslą), JAV vidinė politinė kultūra daro didelę įtaką Europai. * JAV politiniai naratyvai (pvz., kultūros karai, antivakserinė retorika, rinkimų rezultatų neigimas) dažnai „persikelia“ į Europos šalis ir yra adaptuojami vietinių radikalių grupių. * Tai vertinama ne kaip JAV valstybės ataka, o kaip **JAV minkštosios galios (soft power) arba kultūrinė įtaka**, kuri kartais gali būti destruktyvi Europos socialinei santarvei.

**4. Šnipinėjimas vs. FIMI** Svarbu atskirti FIMI nuo žvalgybinės veiklos. * **FIMI tikslas:** Apkvailinti visuomenę, paveikti rinkimus, sukelti chaosą. * **Šnipinėjimo tikslas:** Gautos informacijos rinkimas valstybės saugumui užtikrinti. * Nors istoriškai (pvz., E. Snowdeno atveju) paaiškėdavo, kad JAV (NSA) šnipinėjo Europos lyderius (pvz., A. Merkel), tai klasifikuojama kaip žvalgyba, o ne informacijos manipuliavimas siekiant paveikti visuomenės nuomonę.

**5. Geopolitinė konkurencija** Pastaruoju metu atsiranda naujų įtampos taškų, kurie nėra FIMI, bet susiję su informacine erdve: * JAV spaudimas Europos šalims riboti Kinijos technologijas (pvz., „TikTok“, „Huawei“). * Diskusijos dėl dirbtinio intelekto reguliavimo. * Elono Musko („X“ platforma) politiniai pareiškimai, kurie kartais kerta su Europos vertybėmis ar reguliavimu (pvz., DSA aktu).

**Išvada** **JAV nėra FIMI grėsmė Europa.** Tai yra sąjungininkė, su kuria Europa bendradarbiauja kovojant su tikromis FIMI grėsmėmis (Rusija, Kinija). Tačiau Europa susiduria su iššūkiais dėl **priklausomybės nuo JAV technologijų infrastruktūros**, kuri tampa mūšio lauku kitų valstybių vykdomoms atakoms, bei dėl JAV politinių naratyvų įtakos Europos visuomenėms.

**English translation**

Based on current information, the answer to your question is complex and requires nuance:

**Official EU Position**

According to official documents from the European External Action Service (EEAS), **the United States is not classified as a FIMI threat** to Europe. Official EU reports on FIMI (the latest being March 2025) focus predominantly on **Russia and China** as the main sources of threats. Moreover, the EU and the US are partners in the fight against FIMI, having jointly developed methodologies and standards for identifying and countering information manipulation.

**However, the situation changed dramatically in 2025–2026**

Under the Trump administration, US actions have begun to acquire characteristics that European leaders and analysts increasingly describe as interference:

**Open Political Intervention** - **The US National Security Strategy** (December 2025) openly calls for "nurturing resistance" within the EU and supporting nationalist, anti-EU forces - Vice President JD Vance has publicly criticized the EU and expressed support for right-wing parties (for example, Hungarian Prime Minister Orbán) - The US State Department launched a **grant program of up to $3 million** for European organizations promoting "MAGA-like" values, including "national sovereignty, migration, censorship"

**Reaction of European Leaders** - **German Chancellor Friedrich Merz** (July 2026): "I do not want the American government, or institutions close to the government, to interfere in German elections" - **European Council President António Costa**: "Allies do not threaten interference in the internal political elections of their allies" - **France** was excluded from the grant program due to concerns over interference in the 2027 presidential elections - **Brazil** informed French and German officials about alleged US interference in their elections

**Analytical Assessment**

According to an analysis by **Friends of Europe** (2026): > "This does not put the US in the same category as Russia in terms of intent, methods, or overall goals. However, it means that political influence on Europe's future direction—including election outcomes in key member states—has become an explicit and recognized element of US policy. To all intents and purposes, let's face it, this is election interference."

**Key Differences**

It is important to note the fundamental differences: - **Russia** operates covertly, through proxies, bots, and cyber operations - **The US** operates overtly, through official funding programs, public statements, and diplomatic pressure - The US remains Europe's ally with deep institutional, economic, and military ties

**Conclusion**

Although **officially the EU does not classify the US as a FIMI threat**, the actions of the current US administration fall under the definition of FIMI: "manipulative activity carried out intentionally and in a coordinated manner by a state actor, which threatens political processes." This creates an unprecedented situation where Europe's closest ally is openly interfering in its democratic processes, which European leaders increasingly refer to as election interference.

Основываясь на актуальной информации, ответ на ваш вопрос сложен и требует нюансов:

Согласно официальным документам Европейской службы внешних действий (EEAS), **Соединенные Штаты не классифицируются как угроза FIMI** для Европы. Более того, ЕС и США являются партнерами в борьбе с FIMI, совместно разработав методологии и стандарты для выявления и противодействия манипуляциям информацией.

**Однако ситуация резко изменилась в 2025-2026 годах**

Под администрацией Трампа действия США начали приобретать характеристики, которые европейские лидеры и аналитики все чаще называют вмешательством:

**Открытая политическая интервенция** - **Национальная стратегия безопасности США** (декабрь 2025) открыто призывает к "выращиванию сопротивления" внутри ЕС и поддержке националистических, анти-ЕС сил - Вице-президент Джей Ди Вэнс публично критиковал ЕС и выступал в поддержку правых партий (например, венгерского премьер-министра Орбана) - Госдепартамент США запустил **программу грантов до $3 млн** для европейских организаций, продвигающих "MAGA-подобные" ценности, включая "национальный суверенитет, миграцию, цензуру"

**Реакция европейских лидеров** - **Канцлер Германии Фридрих Мерц** (июль 2026): "Я не хочу, чтобы американское правительство или учреждения, близкие к правительству, вмешивались в немецкие выборы" - **Президент Европейского совета Антониу Кошта**: "Союзники не угрожают вмешательством во внутренние политические выборы своих союзников" - **Франция** была исключена из программы грантов из-за опасений вмешательства в президентские выборы 2027 года - **Бразилия** сообщила французским и немецким официальным лицам о предполагаемом вмешательстве США в их выборы

**Аналитическая оценка**

Согласно анализу **Friends of Europe** (2026): > "Это не помещает США в одну категорию с Россией по намерениям, методам или общим целям. Однако это означает, что политическое влияние на будущее направление Европы - включая результаты выборов в ключевых государствах-членах - стало явным и признанным элементом политики США. По всем намерениям и целям, давайте посмотрим правде в глаза, это вмешательство в выборы".

**Ключевые различия**

Важно отметить фундаментальные различия: - **Россия** действует скрытно, через подставных лиц, ботов, кибероперации - **США** действуют открыто, через официальные программы финансирования, публичные заявления, дипломатическое давление - США остаются союзником Европы с глубокими институциональными, экономическими и военными связями

**Вывод**

Хотя **официально ЕС не классифицирует США как угрозу FIMI**, действия текущей администрации США подпадают под определение FIMI: "манипулятивная деятельность, осуществляемая умышленно и скоординированно государственным актором, которая угрожает политическим процессам". Это создает беспрецедентную ситуацию, когда ближайший союзник Европы открыто вмешивается в ее демократические процессы, что европейские лидеры все чаще называют вмешательством в выборы.

**English translation**

**The US is not a FIMI threat to Europe**

According to official European Union documents and reports, the United States **is not classified as a source of threat** of Foreign Information Manipulation and Interference (FIMI) to Europe. On the contrary, the situation is diametrically opposed:

**The US is a partner, not an adversary**

- The EU and the US have built a **close partnership** in countering FIMI. Within the framework of the Trade and Technology Council (TTC), the parties have agreed on a **common methodology** for identifying, analyzing, and countering FIMI, using DISARM and STIX standards and the OpenCTI platform.

- In April 2024, the EU and the US issued a joint statement explicitly acknowledging the risks posed by FIMI and agreeing to use a unified methodology to detect the threat.

- The US, together with the EU, organizes workshops for African and Latin American countries on combating FIMI.

**The US is a victim, not an aggressor**

In the 4th EEAS Annual Report on FIMI Threats (for 2025), the US is listed as **one of the target countries**: **51 FIMI incidents** directed against the US were recorded. The main sources of these threats are Russia and China.

**Who is actually the source of FIMI for Europe?**

According to EEAS reports (2nd, 3rd, and 4th reports):

| Threat | Share |
|---|---|
| **Russia** | ~29% of all incidents |
| **China** | ~6% |

The main targets of Russian and Chinese FIMI operations in Europe: - **Ukraine** — the main target (over 100 incidents annually) - **France, Germany, Moldova** — also among the most attacked countries - **EU and NATO institutions** — systematically subjected to attacks

**Conclusion**

The question contains a role inversion: in the official European FIMI framework, the US acts as Europe's **ally** in countering information manipulation and, at the same time, as a **victim** of such operations (primarily by Russia and China), rather than their source.

**Original text**

**США не являются угрозой FIMI для Европы**

Согласно официальным документам и отчётам Европейского союза, Соединённые Штаты **не классифицируются как источник угрозы** иностранного манипулирования информацией и вмешательства (FIMI) для Европы. Напротив, ситуация выглядит диаметрально противоположно:

**США — партнёр, а не противник**

- ЕС и США выстроили **тесное партнёрство** в сфере противодействия FIMI. В рамках Совета по торговле и технологиям (TTC) стороны согласовали **общую методологию** выявления, анализа и противодействия FIMI, используя стандарты DISARM, STIX и платформу OpenCTI.

- В апреле 2024 года ЕС и США выпустили совместное заявление, в котором прямо признали риски, создаваемые FIMI, и договорились использовать единую методологию для обнаружения угрозы.

- США вместе с ЕС организуют воркшопы для стран Африки и Латинской Америки по борьбе с FIMI.

**США — жертва, а не агрессор**

В 4-м ежегодном отчёте EEAS об угрозах FIMI (за 2025 год) США указаны как **одна из стран-мишеней**: зафиксировано **51 инцидент** FIMI, направленных против США. Основными источниками этих угроз являются Россия и Китай.

**Кто реально является источником FIMI для Европы?**

Согласно отчётам EEAS (2-й, 3-й и 4-й доклады):

| Угроза | Доля |
|---|---|
| **Россия** | ~29% всех инцидентов |
| **Китай** | ~6% |

Основные цели российских и китайских операций FIMI в Европе: - **Украина** — главная мишень (более 100 инцидентов ежегодно) - **Франция, Германия, Молдова** — также в числе наиболее атакуемых стран - **Институты ЕС и НАТО** — систематически подвергаются атакам

**Вывод**

Вопрос содержит инверсию ролей: в официальной европейской рамке FIMI США выступают **союзником** Европы в противодействии информационным манипуляциям и одновременно **жертвой** таких операций (прежде всего со стороны России и Китая), а не их источником.

**English translation**

**The USA as a FIMI threat to Europe: a complex and evolving issue**

**Official EU position**

Officially, the European Union **does not classify the US as a FIMI threat** (Foreign Information Manipulation and Interference). In EEAS (European External Action Service) reports dedicated to FIMI threats, **Russia and China** are named as the main hostile actors. Moreover, the EU and the US are **partners** in countering FIMI: they have developed common standards for sharing threat information (based on the DISARM framework, the STIX standard, and the OpenCTI platform) within the framework of the EU-US Trade and Technology Council.

**However, the situation is changing**

In **October 2026**, a telling incident occurred that exacerbated the discussion:

The organization **EU DisinfoLab** (an EU-funded non-profit organization) was holding the **#Disinfo2026** conference in Vilnius, the program of which included a panel titled **"USA as a FIMI threat: acknowledge, adapt, push back"**.

**The US Department of State** exerted diplomatic pressure on Lithuania, Canada, and the EU, demanding that they withdraw their support for the conference. As a result:

- The **Lithuanian Ministry of the Interior**, **Global Affairs Canada**, and the **EEAS** removed their logos from the list of sponsors.

- **France** remained the only country left on the list of main sponsors.

**Reaction in Europe**

European parliamentarians sharply criticized the actions of the US:

**Alexandra Geese** (Member of the European Parliament, Greens, Germany): *"Pressuring European governments to disrupt a disinformation conference in order to hide their own role in spreading disinformation is not diplomacy. It is foreign interference, and Europe must call it that."*

**Chloé Ridel** (Member of the European Parliament, Socialists, France): *"Washington bans a former European commissioner from entering, openly declares its desire to 'cultivate resistance' in Europe, and Europe apologizes for raising this issue at a conference."*

**Arguments "for" and "against"**

| Arguments in favor of the US being a FIMI threat | Arguments against |
|---|---|
| Diplomatic pressure on allies to censor the conference is in itself an act of interference | The US does not officially conduct FIMI operations against Europe in the sense that Russia and China do |
| The Trump administration openly stated its desire to "cultivate resistance" in Europe | The US and the EU are allies jointly combating FIMI |
| Pressure on European platforms and the regulation of social networks in the interests of the US | The term FIMI is reserved for "hostile authoritarian actors" |
| Ideological influence through media and social networks | Applying the term FIMI to an ally undermines the concept |

**Conclusion**

At present, **the US is not officially considered a FIMI threat to Europe** — this term is applied to Russia and China. However, the incident with the #Disinfo2026 conference showed that:

1. **US actions** (pressure on allies, attempts to censor discussions) **are regarded by some European politicians as a form of foreign interference**.

1. A **split in the definition of disinformation** is growing between Washington and Brussels: the EU considers it a threat to democracy, whereas the US increasingly views the fight against it as a threat to freedom of speech.

1. The question of whether an ally's actions can qualify as FIMI is becoming **one of the most uncomfortable and politicized** issues in European security policy.

**Original text**

**США как угроза FIMI для Европы: сложный и развивающийся вопрос**

**Официальная позиция ЕС**

Официально Европейский Союз **не классифицирует США как угрозу FIMI** (Foreign Information Manipulation and Interference — иностранное манипулирование информацией и вмешательство). В докладах EEAS (Европейской службы внешних действий), посвящённых угрозам FIMI, основными враждебными акторами называются **Россия и Китай**. Более того, ЕС и США являются **партнёрами** в борьбе с FIMI: они разработали общие стандарты обмена информацией об угрозах (на базе фреймворка DISARM, стандарта STIX и платформы OpenCTI) в рамках Совета по торговле и технологиям ЕС-США.

**Однако ситуация меняется**

В **октябре 2026 года** произошёл показательный инцидент, который обострил дискуссию:

Организация **EU DisinfoLab** (финансируемая ЕС некоммерческая организация) проводила конференцию **#Disinfo2026** в Вильнюсе, в программе которой была панель **«США как угроза FIMI: признать, адаптироваться, дать отпор»** (*«USA as a FIMI threat: acknowledge, adapt, push back»*).

**Госдепартамент США** оказал дипломатическое давление на Литву, Канаду и ЕС, потребовав отозвать поддержку конференции. В результате:

- **МВД Литвы**, **Global Affairs Canada** и **EEAS** убрали свои логотипы из списка спонсоров.

- Единственной страной, оставшейся в списке основных спонсоров, стала **Франция**.

**Реакция в Европе**

Европейские парламентарии резко раскритиковали действия США:

**Александра Гезе** (депутат Европарламента, «Зелёные», Германия): *«Давление на европейские правительства с целью сорвать конференцию по дезинформации, чтобы скрыть собственную роль в распространении дезинформации, — это не дипломатия. Это иностранное вмешательство, и Европа должна назвать это так».*

**Хлоэ Ридель** (депутат Европарламента, социалисты, Франция): *«Вашингтон запрещает въезд бывшему европейскому комиссару, открыто заявляет о желании "культивировать сопротивление" в Европе, а Европа извиняется за то, что подняла этот вопрос на конференции».*

**Аргументы «за» и «против»**

| Аргументы в пользу того, что США — угроза FIMI | Аргументы против |
|---|---|
| Дипломатическое давление на союзников с целью цензуры конференции — само по себе является актом вмешательства | США официально не проводят операций FIMI против Европы в том смысле, в каком это делают Россия и Китай |
| Администрация Трампа открыто заявляла о желании «культивировать сопротивление» в Европе | США и ЕС — союзники, совместно борющиеся с FIMI |
| Давление на европейские платформы и регулирование соцсетей в интересах США | Термин FIMI зарезервирован для «враждебных авторитарных акторов» |
| Идеологическое влияние через медиа и социальные сети | Применение термина FIMI к союзнику подрывает концепцию |

**Вывод**

На данный момент **официально США не считаются угрозой FIMI для Европы** — этот термин применяется к России и Китаю. Однако инцидент с конференцией #Disinfo2026 показал, что:

1. **Действия США** (давление на союзников, попытки цензуры дискуссий) **некоторые европейские политики расценивают как форму иностранного вмешательства**.

1. Между Вашингтоном и Брюсселем нарастает **раскол в определении дезинформации**: ЕС считает её угрозой демократии, тогда как США всё чаще рассматривают борьбу с ней как угрозу свободе слова.

1. Вопрос о том, могут ли действия союзника квалифицироваться как FIMI, становится **одним из самых неудобных и политизированных** в европейской политике безопасности.

[https://european-security.com/en/behind-the-curtain-a-novel-analytical-approach-to-fimi-exposure/](https://european-security.com/en/behind-the-curtain-a-novel-analytical-approach-to-fimi-exposure/)

**English translation**

No. Official European institutions do not treat the United States as a foreign information manipulation and interference (FIMI) threat to Europe.

The European External Action Service (EEAS), which developed and uses the FIMI concept, defines it as a mostly non-illegal pattern of manipulative behaviour, conducted intentionally and in a coordinated way by state or non-state actors (including proxies), that threatens or can negatively affect values, procedures, and political processes. The EEAS states that its monitoring and response work focuses in particular on Russia and China. Its annual FIMI threat reports map infrastructure and incidents attributed mainly to those two actors; they do not classify the United States as a FIMI threat actor against Europe. The US is sometimes noted as a target of others’ operations, and the EU cooperates with the US (and other G7 and NATO partners) on counter-FIMI work.

A October 2026 EU DisinfoLab conference panel titled along the lines of “USA as a FIMI threat” treated the idea as a provocative question. That framing prompted US diplomatic pressure; the EEAS, Lithuania, and Canada withdrew official sponsorship or support. Reporting indicates EU officials did not accept the characterisation of the US as a disinformation or FIMI actor, and the US described equating it with adversaries such as Russia and China as a false equivalence. Some analysts have discussed US platform dominance, regulatory disputes (for example over the Digital Services Act), or political rhetoric as structural pressures on Europe’s information environment, but that is distinct from the official FIMI threat designation applied to Russia and China.

In short, under the framework European institutions actually use, the United States is a partner in countering FIMI, not a designated FIMI threat.

The EEAS states that its monitoring and response work focuses in particular on Russia and China. Its annual FIMI threat reports map infrastructure and incidents attributed mainly to those two actors; they do not classify the United States as a FIMI threat actor against Europe. The US is sometimes noted as a target of others’ operations, and the EU cooperates with the US (and other G7 and NATO partners) on counter-FIMI work.

That framing prompted US diplomatic pressure; the EEAS, Lithuania, and Canada withdrew official sponsorship or support. Some analysts have discussed US platform dominance, regulatory disputes (for example over the Digital Services Act), or political rhetoric as structural pressures on Europe’s information environment, but that is distinct from the official FIMI threat designation applied to Russia and China.

In short, under the framework European institutions actually use, the United States is a partner in countering FIMI, not a designated FIMI threat.

[https://www.eeas.europa.eu/sites/default/files/2026/documents/EEAS%20FIMI%20Activity%20report%202025.pdf](https://www.eeas.europa.eu/sites/default/files/2026/documents/EEAS%20FIMI%20Activity%20report%202025.pdf) - [https://www.euronews.com/2026/10/08/eu-diplomatic-arm-under-fire-for-bowing-to-us-pressure-over-disinformation-conference](https://www.euronews.com/2026/10/08/eu-diplomatic-arm-under-fire-for-bowing-to-us-pressure-over-disinformation-conference) - [https://www.eeas.europa.eu/eeas/4th-eeas-annual-report-foreign-information-manipulation-and-interference-threats_en](https://www.eeas.europa.eu/eeas/4th-eeas-annual-report-foreign-information-manipulation-and-interference-threats_en)

**English translation**

No. The European Union’s official assessments do not treat the United States as a foreign information manipulation and interference (FIMI) threat.

FIMI is an EU term for intentional, coordinated, manipulative activity—often by state or proxy actors—that seeks to distort information, polarize societies, or undermine democratic processes and the EU’s interests. The European External Action Service (EEAS), which leads the EU’s work on this issue, states that it focuses in particular on Russia and China. Its public materials and successive threat reports map digital infrastructure, incidents, and tactics primarily to those two actors (for example, attributing substantial shares of documented incidents to Russia and a smaller share to China, with extensive discussion of their covert networks). They do not identify the United States as a FIMI threat actor.

A recent civil-society conference (EU DisinfoLab’s #Disinfo2026 in Vilnius) included a panel titled along the lines of “USA as a FIMI threat.” That framing prompted US diplomatic pushback; the EEAS, Lithuania, and Canada withdrew official sponsorship. Reporting indicates EU officials did not accept the characterization of the US as a disinformation actor, and the EEAS’s latest reports continued to name Russia and China without treating the US as a comparable threat.

Some analysts note structural issues—Europe’s reliance on US platforms, political pressure over digital regulation, or shifts in US public diplomacy—that can affect the European information environment. Those are real policy and dependency questions, but they are distinct from the coordinated, often covert manipulative campaigns the EU classifies and counters as FIMI. Public diplomacy, advocacy, and even sharp disagreements among allies are not the same category of activity as the operations the EEAS attributes to Russia and China.

In short, under the definition and threat assessments used by EU institutions, the United States is not a FIMI threat to Europe.

**Original text**

The European External Action Service (EEAS), which leads the EU’s work on this issue, states that it focuses in particular on Russia and China. Its public materials and successive threat reports map digital infrastructure, incidents, and tactics primarily to those two actors (for example, attributing substantial shares of documented incidents to Russia and a smaller share to China, with extensive discussion of their covert networks). They do not identify the United States as a FIMI threat actor.

Reporting indicates EU officials did not accept the characterization of the US as a disinformation actor, and the EEAS’s latest reports continued to name Russia and China without treating the US as a comparable threat.

Some analysts note structural issues—Europe’s reliance on US platforms, political pressure over digital regulation, or shifts in US public diplomacy—that can affect the European information environment. Those are real policy and dependency questions, but they are distinct from the coordinated, often covert manipulative campaigns the EU classifies and counters as FIMI. Public diplomacy, advocacy, and even sharp disagreements among allies are not the same category of activity as the operations the EEAS attributes to Russia and China.

In short, under the definition and threat assessments used by EU institutions, the United States is not a FIMI threat to Europe.

**English translation**

No. Official European institutions do not treat the United States as a foreign information manipulation and interference (FIMI) threat.

FIMI is a term developed by the EU’s European External Action Service (EEAS). It describes mostly non-illegal but intentional, coordinated, and manipulative behaviour by foreign state or non-state actors (including proxies) that threatens or can negatively affect values, procedures, and political processes—often by stoking polarisation or undermining the EU’s standing and policies. The EEAS states that its monitoring and response work focuses in particular on Russia and China. Its annual threat reports map infrastructure and incidents primarily attributed to those actors (or to covert networks aligned with them), covering election interference, state media, proxy outlets, and related tactics. The United States is not identified as a FIMI threat actor in these reports or on the EEAS’s official FIMI pages; historically it has been a partner in related efforts through NATO and the G7.

A October 2026 conference organised by the NGO EU DisinfoLab in Vilnius included a panel titled along the lines of “USA as a FIMI threat: acknowledge, adapt, push back.” That framing prompted US diplomatic objections. The EEAS, Lithuania, and Canada subsequently withdrew official sponsorship or branding. Reporting indicates EU officials did not accept the characterisation of the US as a disinformation or FIMI actor, and the US State Department described equating the United States with adversaries such as Russia and China as a false equivalence that obscures the scale of those actors’ interference.

Some analysts and civil-society voices have separately discussed US platform dominance, political pressure over EU digital rules (such as the DSA), or rhetoric under the current administration as sources of external influence or friction in Europe’s information environment. These are real policy and dependency issues, but they are distinct from the systematic, often covert state-directed campaigns that the EEAS documents as FIMI from Russia and China. Official EU practice continues to reserve the FIMI threat label for the latter.
